### 37.17 Backend Authorization Enforcement (closes Mistakes 1 & 2 — frontend trust, broken access control)

```
HARD RULE: no protected operation may be authorized by frontend state. A hidden button, a
disabled control, a client-side route guard, or an account-aware UI element is a UX
convenience, never a security boundary. Every protected request — Admin Centre actions,
Institution Hub access, verification decisions, credential issuance, organisation
management, recruiter capabilities, marketplace management, payments and refunds,
commission configuration, Trust Score-affecting actions, user suspension, data exports,
messaging permissions, private file access, and API access — must be independently
authorized at the backend, every time, regardless of what the requesting client displayed.

For every such request the backend must resolve, server-side: WHO is requesting (§37.6
RBAC), WHAT action, WHICH resource, WHY they're authorized for that specific resource
(ownership/scope check — extends §37.5's existing RLS matrix), and UNDER WHICH account,
organisation, institution, or tenant they're currently operating (§50.2's Data Domains).

Client-submitted values for the following fields are never trusted as-is — they are
resolved or independently validated server-side against authoritative state: role,
is_admin, is_verified, trust_score, verification_status, price, commission, payment_status,
owner_id, organization_id, institution_id, seller_id, moderation_status. A request that
includes any of these fields in its payload has that portion of the payload ignored in
favor of the server's own record, not merged with client input (closes mass-assignment).
```

### 37.18 Business Logic & Workflow Integrity (closes Mistake 3 — securing workflows, not
just endpoints)

```
Individually-secure endpoints do not make a workflow secure. The following multi-step
flows already exist elsewhere in this document and each one requires an authoritative
server-side state machine governing valid transitions — not client-sequenced API calls:

- Verification (§12.4's timeline: submitted → received → institution_notified →
  under_review → approved → verified/rejected) — a request can only advance one valid
  transition at a time; a rejected request cannot be silently republished by calling a
  different endpoint, and an 'academic_affiliation' verification (§4.4) can never
  transition into an 'academic' credential without a separate, distinct verification event
- Self-tagging and collaborator attribution (§11.3 SelfTagRequest) — a claimed
  contribution only becomes attributed CV/Portfolio evidence after the original poster (or
  an authorized organisation) explicitly accepts it; there is no path that skips this
  approval, including calling the CV-update endpoint directly
- Commerce (§38.6b's lifecycle, now idempotent per §37.20) — an order cannot be marked
  paid by the client, a seller cannot receive payout before fulfilment confirmation, a
  refund cannot be issued while the buyer retains undisclosed benefit, and one transaction
  cannot generate two commission calculations
- Trust Score changes (§13) — only ever written by the server-side formula in §13.2/§50.1,
  triggered by a verified event; no client request can set a Trust Score value directly,
  and TrustScoreFormulaChangeLog (§50.1) makes any authorized change auditable after the fact
- Invitations and one-time tokens (§8.5 email verification, §42.3 org invitations,
  §12.4 verification links) — single-use, expiring, and rejected on reuse
```

### 37.19 External API, Webhook & SSRF Trust Boundaries (closes Mistakes 4 & 5)

```
PROFILE integrates with authentication, payment, email, storage, AI, and institutional
providers (§16.7 VXI APIs, §38.6 PROFILE Payments, §48.11 AI Gateway) plus government and
university systems via the country adapters (§16.6). None of these are trusted merely for
claiming to be the expected sender:

- Every inbound webhook is signature-verified against the provider's published scheme,
  checked for expiry/replay, and processed idempotently (§37.20) — a webhook that fails
  verification is rejected and logged, never processed "to be safe"
- Institutional API responses (§16.4 Matching Engine) are schema-validated before being
  treated as a verification result — a malformed or unexpected response routes to manual
  review, it never silently auto-verifies
- Any PROFILE feature that fetches a user-or-externally-supplied URL — evidence links,
  portfolio imports, remote media in Activity uploads (§4.3), document imports, the camera/
  scan pipeline's remote-source option (§49.7a) — is an SSRF surface. These fetches run
  through isolated outbound infrastructure that blocks localhost, loopback and private
  network ranges, cloud metadata endpoints, and validates the destination again after any
  redirect — an allowlist is used wherever the set of legitimate destinations is knowable
  in advance (e.g. known institutional API hosts, §16.6)
```

### 37.20 Race Conditions, Replay & Idempotent Financial Flows (closes Mistake 9)

```
A valid request succeeding once does not mean it should succeed twice. §38.6b's Commerce
Transaction Lifecycle, §38.9's institution verification-fee flow, and §16's verification
approvals all carry this requirement:

- Every financial mutation (checkout, payout, refund, commission split, institution
  verification fee) carries a server-generated idempotency key; a retried or duplicated
  request with the same key returns the original result rather than repeating the effect
- Database-level unique constraints and atomic transactions back every "only once" business
  rule — a double-click checkout, a duplicated webhook delivery, and a race between two
  simultaneous role-change requests must each resolve to one consistent outcome, enforced by
  the database, not by client-side debouncing
- Financial truth is read from the server's own transaction record and the payment
  provider's verified state (§37.19) — never calculated or finalized in frontend JavaScript,
  including PROFILE's own commission (restates §38.6b's existing server-side rule, now tied
  explicitly to this section)
```

### 37.21 File Upload Deep Validation (extends §11.4/§49.7a upload pipeline, §27.2's
existing virus scanning — closes the remaining gaps in Mistake 7)

```
§27.2 already requires upload sanitisation and virus scanning; this closes what that
requirement didn't yet make explicit. Every file — whether from Record Work (§11.4), the
camera/scan pipeline (§49.7a), a message attachment (§20), or a credential upload (§12.5) —
is validated by actual server-side file-type inspection, never by trusting its extension or
browser-reported MIME type. Archives are decompressed under size and depth limits specific
to preventing zip-bomb and archive-traversal exploitation before any content inside them is
processed. Stored files receive randomized, non-guessable object names — a private
credential or portfolio file is never reachable merely because someone guesses or
enumerates its URL; private downloads are served through signed, time-limited URLs with an
authorization check on every request, not a one-time check at upload time.
```

### 37.22 Dependency, Supply Chain & Deployment Hardening (closes Mistake 10)

```
Before any dependency is added, it is checked for necessity and active maintenance —
generated code accumulating unnecessary or abandoned packages is treated as technical debt,
not a neutral default. Lockfiles, dependency vulnerability scanning, and secret scanning run
in CI/CD before deploy; CI/CD credentials themselves follow least privilege rather than a
single all-access deploy key. Production configuration sets Content-Security-Policy,
HSTS, MIME-sniffing protection (X-Content-Type-Options), clickjacking protection
(X-Frame-Options/frame-ancestors), a referrer policy, a permissions policy, and CORS scoped
to PROFILE's actual known origins — never a wildcard origin on any endpoint that reads
authenticated data. Compiling successfully is not a deployment gate; the checklist at
§37.25 is.
```

### 37.23 Admin Centre Step-Up & Accountability (extends §26, §37.11 — closes the gap this
document never specified despite §26 already restricting Admin Centre to named roles)

```
§26 already restricts the Admin Centre to Super Admins, Admins, Verification Officers, and
Compliance Officers. What was missing is what happens once someone in those roles is
authenticated: highly destructive actions — permanent account suspension, mass data export,
trust-score formula changes (§50.1), credential revocation at scale — require step-up
re-authentication at the moment of the action, not just a valid existing session. Every such
action requires a recorded reason code and writes to the same immutable SecurityAuditEntry
(§37.7) already required platform-wide. A single compromised ordinary admin credential must
not be sufficient, alone, to take an irreversible destructive action across the ecosystem.
```

### 37.24 AI Authorization Boundary & Prompt Injection Defense (extends §48.0's existing
AI_ENGINE_PRINCIPLES — closes a gap those principles implied but never stated against
adversarial input specifically)

```
§48.0 already establishes that AI cannot bypass verification and cannot convert an
inference into a verified fact. This extends that boundary against deliberate manipulation:
AI is never the authorization authority for verifying a credential, approving an
institution, changing a role, authorizing a payment, or finalizing a Trust Score — those
remain server-side, rule-governed operations (§37.17–§37.20) that AI can at most recommend
into, never execute directly. Uploaded documents and messages processed by the AI Gateway
(§48.11) are treated as untrusted input for prompt-injection purposes — instructions
embedded inside a resume, a message, or a scanned document (§49.7a) cannot cause the AI to
disclose another user's private data, invoke a tool outside its intended scope, or bypass
the access controls in §37.17. Model context is scoped per-request to the requesting user's
own authorized data (§50.2 Data Domains) — never assembled from a broader pool that could
leak across users.
```

### 37.25 Final Platform Validation Checklist

```
✓ Every route resolves correctly — no 404 for a registered feature
✓ No dead links, dead buttons, or blank screens
✓ No placeholder functionality
✓ Every upload succeeds and is retrievable
✓ Every verification workflow completes end to end
✓ Every institution schema functions
✓ Every Trust Center workflow is operational
✓ Every Opportunity workflow is complete
✓ Every Message workflow functions
✓ Every privacy control works as configured
✓ Every RLS policy is intentional and least-privilege
✓ Every role has documented, non-escalating permissions
✓ Password security complies with §37.2
✓ Profile data is encrypted at rest and in transit
✓ User sessions are secure and revocable
✓ Every module defines its eight privacy-by-design items before launch (§50.10.17) —
  ownership, visibility, download, sharing, verification, export, retention, legal hold
```

**Adversarial testing gate (extends the checklist above — a feature working correctly for
a legitimate user is not the same as being security-complete):**

```
✓ User A cannot read User B's private resource by changing an ID (§37.17 IDOR check)
✓ An individual account cannot call an institution-only or organisation-only endpoint
✓ An organisation cannot verify itself where §16 prohibits self-verification
✓ A seller cannot change a price after payment authorization (§37.18)
✓ The same webhook delivered twice cannot trigger two payouts (§37.19, §37.20)
✓ An unverified institution cannot create authoritative credentials
✓ A private file cannot be accessed without authorization, even with a guessed URL (§37.21)
✓ An admin-only action cannot be invoked directly by a normal user bypassing the Admin
  Centre UI (§37.17, §37.23)
✓ A verification or invitation token cannot be reused after redemption (§37.18)
✓ A user cannot manipulate their own Trust Score via a crafted request (§37.17, §37.18)
✓ Uploaded content cannot execute in another user's browser (§37.21, existing §27.2)
✓ A prompt embedded in an uploaded document or message cannot cause the AI to disclose
  another user's data or invoke a tool outside its scope (§37.24)

This gate is run against negative and malicious scenarios specifically, not only happy
paths — per this section's own governing rule, a feature is security-complete only after
testing shows unauthorized users and malicious input cannot make it behave outside its
intended rules.
```

### 37.26 Security & Trust Center — User-Facing Consolidation (extends §7.2–7.4, §37.3,
§37.7–37.10, §16.7, §48.10, §50.1, §50.3 — one new user-facing surface; §37.1–§37.25
already define the backend architecture this surface sits on top of, and are unchanged)

#### 37.26.0 What This Section Is, and Isn't

```
§37.1–37.25 is already a thorough backend security build. This section is the small number
of genuinely missing pieces, plus the single user-facing surface — called the Security &
Trust Center — that makes §7, §25, §27, §37, and §50.1–50.2 legible to an actual user as
one coherent place, rather than several correct but scattered subsystems. Nothing below
duplicates an existing schema, rule, or backend control.
```

#### 37.26.1 Authentication Method Gaps (extends §37.3 MFA_METHODS — additive)

```typescript
// EXTENDS §37.3's MFA_METHODS array — the existing entries are unchanged; these are new,
// plus one reclassification the platform's own risk tiering already implies.
const MFA_METHODS_V2_ADDITIONS = [
  'hardware_security_key',              // FIDO2/WebAuthn hardware key — distinct from a
                                          // software passkey; §50.2's MFA_POLICY_BY_RISK_TIER
                                          // already requires "phishing-resistant hardware-backed
                                          // MFA" for super_admin, which presumes this method exists
  'biometric_platform_authenticator',    // device-native biometric unlock feeding the existing
                                          // WebAuthn platform-authenticator flow — PROFILE never
                                          // stores a biometric template itself; the device's own
                                          // secure enclave does, and PROFILE receives only a
                                          // pass/fail assertion
  'login_approval_push',                 // a "was this you?" push confirmation to an existing
                                          // trusted device (§37.3 TrustedDeviceManagement),
                                          // offered as an alternative to SMS OTP once a user has
                                          // ≥2 trusted devices registered
];
// RECLASSIFICATION: §37.3's 'passkeys_webauthn_future_ready' entry becomes live, not
// future-ready — §50.2 already mandates it for three of four risk tiers, so the platform's
// own governance assumed this was shipped. This is a status change, not a schema change.

// NEW — configurable password expiration, OFF by default for individuals (forced rotation
// is a known anti-pattern that pushes users toward weaker passwords), available only as an
// institution/enterprise-configurable policy, same opt-in pattern as §38.9's institution
// commerce toggles:
interface PasswordExpirationPolicy {
  enabled: boolean;                      // false by default for every account type
  intervalDays?: number;                 // set only when an institution/enterprise account opts in
  appliesTo: 'institution_or_enterprise_accounts_only';
}
```

#### 37.26.2 Session Detail Fields (extends §7.4 SessionManagement — display fields only)

```typescript
// EXTENDS §7.4's Session type with the display fields shown in the Security & Trust Center
// UI. sessionRevocation (§7.4) already covers "logout this device / all devices" at the
// capability level; this only names the fields shown per session so that capability is
// actually visible to the user.
interface SessionDisplayFields {
  browser: string; deviceType: string; operatingSystem: string;
  approximateLocation: string; ipAddressMasked: string;
  lastActivity: Date; sessionDurationMinutes: number;
}
```

#### 37.26.3 Security Timeline (new — renders §37.7 SecurityAuditEntry to the user; no second log)

```
GAP CLOSED: §37.7 defines SecurityAuditEntry and states logs are "searchable by admins,"
but nothing let the account owner see their own security history. This is a user-facing,
read-only, chronological filter over the same SecurityAuditEntry stream — the same pattern
§50.5 already uses to render TrustTimelineEntry as the Trust Passport's visible history.
```

```typescript
// EXTENDS §37.7 SecurityAuditEntry.action union — additive, backward compatible.
type SecurityAuditActionV2 = SecurityAuditEntry['action']
  | 'passkey_or_hardware_key_registered' | 'passkey_or_hardware_key_removed'
  | 'connected_application_authorized' | 'connected_application_revoked';  // ties to §37.26.5

const SECURITY_TIMELINE_EXPORT = { formats: ['machine_readable_json', 'pdf'] };
// Reuses §50.3's DATA_EXPORT_FORMATS exactly — not a new export format list.
```

#### 37.26.4 Recovery Center — Trusted Contact Path (extends §7.3 RECOVERY_PATHS — adds one path)

```typescript
// EXTENDS §7.3's RECOVERY_PATHS — path 5 is new; paths 1–4 are unchanged.
const RECOVERY_PATH_5 = {
  path: 5,
  method: 'Verified trusted contact confirmation (opt-in, configured in advance)',
};
// A user may nominate one or more existing, verified PROFILE accounts as trusted contacts
// ahead of time. Recovery via this path requires BOTH: the trusted contact confirming
// through their own authenticated session, AND the requesting account passing the same
// RECOVERY_ABUSE_PREVENTION checks (§37.8) as every other path. This path cannot be
// invoked cold by someone who simply claims to know the user — it exists only where the
// account owner configured it in advance, same opt-in discipline as §9.7.
```

#### 37.26.5 Connected Applications (new — extends §16.7 VXI APIs, §37.6 RBAC)

```
GAP CLOSED: §16.7 defines the API surface and §48.11 defines the AI Gateway's provider
routing — but nothing previously gave the user a console to see which third parties hold a
live grant against their own account, or to revoke one. This is that console.
```

```typescript
interface ConnectedApplicationGrant {
  id: string; applicationName: string;
  applicationType: 'oauth_app' | 'developer_token' | 'api_key'
                  | 'marketplace_integration' | 'institution_integration'
                  | 'government_integration' | 'ai_provider';
  grantedScopes: string[];               // explicit allow-list, never inferred — §37.6
  issuedAt: Date; lastUsedAt?: Date; status: 'active' | 'revoked';
}
const CONNECTED_APP_ACTIONS = ['view_scopes', 'view_last_usage', 'revoke_access', 'regenerate_token'];
// Revocation is immediate and writes a 'connected_application_revoked' entry to
// SecurityAuditEntry (§37.26.3). An 'ai_provider' grant is specifically the §48.11 AI
// Gateway's per-feature routing made visible where it touches the user's own data.
```

#### 37.26.6 Account Deactivation & Deletion Workflow (new — extends §50.3
RETENTION_AND_DELETION_POLICY and §25 DataRights.delete_my_account)

```
GAP CLOSED: §50.3 specifies the data-layer retention/deletion policy and §25's DataRights
tab lists 'delete_my_account' as a control, but neither specifies the actual confirmation
workflow. This is that workflow — it invokes the existing policy, it does not change it.
```

```typescript
interface AccountDeletionRequest {
  requestedAt: Date;
  confirmationMethod: 'password' | 'password_and_mfa';   // MFA step required if enabled on the account
  recoveryWindowDays: number;                              // account fully recoverable until expiry
  dataExportOfferedBeforeDeletion: true;                    // always — reuses §50.3's DATA_EXPORT_SCOPE
  consequencesShownBeforeConfirmation: string[];            // plain language, e.g. "Your Trust Passport
                                                            // will no longer be shareable"; "Verified
                                                            // credentials tied to institutions remain in
                                                            // institutional records per §46.4"
  status: 'pending_deactivation' | 'deactivated' | 'permanently_deleted' | 'reactivated';
}
// Temporary deactivation is reversible at any time inside the recovery window. Permanent
// deletion follows §50.3's RETENTION_AND_DELETION_POLICY exactly — nothing here overrides it.
```

#### 37.26.7 Anti-Fraud — Step-Up Response (extends §50.1 RISK_RESPONSES — one new response)

```typescript
// EXTENDS §50.1's RISK_RESPONSES = ['automatic_soft_flag','rate_limit',
// 'temporary_trust_freeze','manual_review_queue'] — additive:
const RISK_RESPONSE_STEP_UP = 'require_additional_MFA_factor_for_remainder_of_session';
// Triggered by the same RISK_SIGNALS catalog §50.1 already defines — no new signal is
// introduced, only a named response option not previously in the response set.
```

```typescript

// ('self_paced_courses') and §50.9.7 PROFILE Academy pipeline — not a new content system.
```

```typescript
// EXTENDS §37.10's SUPPORT_ECOSYSTEM array — the existing entries are unchanged.
```

#### 37.26.10 Global Compliance — Additional Standards (extends §27.4, §46.4 — additive)

```typescript
// EXTENDS §27.4's regulations: ['GDPR (EU)', 'NDPR (Nigeria)', 'CCPA (California)']
const COMPLIANCE_REGULATIONS_ADDITIONS = ['UK GDPR', 'PIPEDA (Canada)'];
const SECURITY_STANDARDS_ALIGNMENT = ['ISO 27001', 'SOC 2', 'NIST Cybersecurity Framework'];
// Alignment targets for the security program, not a claim of current certification — per
// §46.0's rule that legal/compliance copy requires qualified counsel review before any
// certification claim is published.
```

#### 37.26.11 Security Copilot — Security Guidance Role (extends §48.0a's Copilot Skills
Framework, §48.10/§50.9.2 PROFILE Advisor — additive, no new agent)

```
This is PROFILE Copilot's Security skill (§48.0a's COPILOT_SKILLS table: "Security
Copilot"), sharing the same CopilotContext, personality, and §48.0 Hard Rules as every
other Copilot skill, operating within the Security & Trust Center specifically. Not a
second AI system. (Naming history: earlier drafts of this document used "PROFILE Copilot"
and "PROFILE Advisor" ambiguously for the same thing before §48.0a resolved PROFILE
Copilot as the umbrella brand and PROFILE Advisor as its Career skill specifically —
this subsection's capability was always a Copilot skill, now correctly named as one.)
```

```typescript
// EXTENDS §48.10's AI_AGENT_CAPABILITIES array, scoped to Security Copilot (§48.0a):
const SECURITY_COPILOT_CAPABILITIES = [
  'explain_a_security_setting_in_plain_language',
  'explain_why_a_specific_trust_score_change_happened',     // reuses §13.3 Transparency Rule
  'explain_a_verification_workflow_stage',                    // reuses §12.4 Verification Timeline
  'surface_a_relevant_§37.26.8_education_module',
  'walk_a_user_through_the_recovery_center_(§37.26.4)',
];
// HARD RULE (restates §48.0/§48.10 unchanged): never performs a sensitive action —
// password change, MFA change, deletion, revocation — without the user's explicit,
// in-the-moment confirmation.
```

# 38. ECONOMIC ENGINE & MONETIZATION ARCHITECTURE

### 38.0 Monetization Philosophy (Design Principle)

```typescript
// Every revenue feature must answer YES to at least one question below.
// If the answer is no to all of them, it is rejected — no exceptions.
const MONETIZATION_ADMISSION_TEST = [
  'Does it help users earn more?',
  'Does it help users get hired?',
  'Does it improve trust?',
  'Does it increase discoverability?',
  'Does it save time?',
  'Does it create economic opportunity?',
  'Does it strengthen the PROFILE ecosystem loop (§1.4)?',
];
// Revenue chain: Identity → Evidence → Verification → Trust → Opportunity → Commerce → Growth
// Monetization enhances value. It never interrupts it. Free users retain a fully usable platform.
```

### 38.1 Professional Services (My Services / Offers)

```typescript
// Naming decision: primary tab is "Professional Services" (enduring capability),
// with "Offers" as a sub-section for time-limited promotions or packages.
```

### 38.6a Commerce Activation — Two Toggles, Not One (extends §19.1, §38.6)

```typescript
interface CommerceSettings {          // one settings object on User/Organisation — not scattered flags
  storeActivated: boolean;             // Toggle 1 — "My Store." Turns on a public page listing
                                        // portfolio items, services, products, or offers. Free or
                                        // subscription-gated per §32.1 — never itself commission-bearing,
                                        // because nothing has been sold yet; it's just a storefront.
  sellingActivated: boolean;           // Toggle 2 — "Accept Payments / Sell through PROFILE." Requires
                                        // storeActivated = true first; cannot be enabled alone. Only this
                                        // toggle activates checkout, escrow, invoices, refunds, payouts,
                                        // and PROFILE's platform fee (§38.6b below).
  acceptedCommerceTermsVersion: string; // must be explicitly re-accepted whenever sellingActivated flips
                                        // true, or whenever §38.6b's applicable fee schedule changes for
                                        // that seller — no silent term changes, ever (consistent with
                                        // §27.4's material-policy-change notice requirement).
}
// The toggle only ACTIVATES the capability. It never sets, overrides, or influences the fee itself —
// see the server-side rule in §38.6b. A user flipping a switch client-side can never change what they
// owe; the switch only unlocks a pipeline whose pricing lives entirely on the server.
```

### 38.6b PROFILE Commerce & Revenue Engine (extends §19.1 Escrow, §38.19 Revenue Matrix —
one configurable engine, not six separate commission systems)

```typescript
interface CommerceTransactionRule {
  transactionType: 'physical_product' | 'digital_product' | 'service'
                  | 'institutional_verification' | 'subscription' | 'advertising' | 'api_usage';
  jurisdiction:  string;    // ISO country/region — different fee, tax, and regulatory rules can apply
  sellerClass:   'individual' | 'organisation' | 'institution';
  feeRule:       { percentage: number; min?: number; max?: number };
  taxRule:       string;
  payoutRule:    string;
  refundRule:    string;
  disputeRule:   string;
}
// §19.1's 90/10 creator split and §38.19's "10% default, 5–10% configurable" remain the DEFAULT
// row this engine falls back to when no more specific rule matches — this gives that existing
// number a proper home and a way to vary by country and seller type, it does not change it.

const COMMERCE_INTEGRITY_RULES = [
  'commission_is_always_calculated_server_side_never_trusted_to_client_state_or_the_toggle_itself',
  'PROFILE_never_attempts_to_monitor_track_or_infer_sales_made_outside_its_own_checkout_pipeline',
                                     // this is the direct answer to "how do we monitor sales" — the honest
                                     // answer is that PROFILE doesn't try to see private, off-platform
                                     // transactions at all. It meters its own pipe, not the person.
  'commission_applies_exclusively_to_transactions_that_completed_the_full_lifecycle_below',
  'every_sellable_item_must_be_listed_through_Marketplace_(§19)_or_Ads_Hub_(§19.2)_—_no_alternate_or_hidden_commerce_surface_may_exist_elsewhere_in_the_product',
];

const COMMERCE_TRANSACTION_LIFECYCLE = [
  'listing', 'checkout', 'payment_authorization', 'escrow_or_processing',
  'fulfilment', 'confirmation', 'commission_split', 'payout', 'receipt',
  'refund_or_dispute_if_necessary',
];
// Every transaction gets a unique ID and moves through this lifecycle server-side; the commission
// split (stage 7) is computed by looking up the CommerceTransactionRule above, never hardcoded
// inline at the point of sale. Every stage transition is idempotent (§37.20) — a duplicate
// webhook, a double-click checkout, or a retried payout request must resolve to the same
// end state exactly once, never a second charge, a second payout, or a second commission split.
```

### 38.6c Completed Transactions as Evidence, With Consent (extends §4.3 Activity — not a
new evidence type)

```typescript
interface CommerceEvidenceConsent {
  transactionId:            string;
  buyerConfirmedCompletion: boolean;
  buyerReview?:             string;
  sellerAddedToPortfolio:   boolean;   // the seller's own choice, never automatic
  financialDetailsExposed:  false;     // always false — amount and commercial terms never leave
                                        // the transaction record itself
}
// A confirmed CommerceEvidenceConsent becomes a normal Activity record (§4.3) exactly like any
// other piece of evidence — closing the loop this whole document keeps insisting on:
// Work → Evidence → Trust → Reputation → Opportunity → Income (§1.4).
```

### 38.6d Orders, Invoices, Payment Methods & Subscriptions (new — extends §19.1 Escrow,
§38.6–§38.6c Commerce Engine, §32.1–32.2 Plans, §37.20's idempotent financial-flow rule —
closes a real gap: this document specifies the payment *rules* thoroughly but never
defined the actual records a user sees as "my orders," "my invoices," "my payment
methods," or "my subscription")

```
GAP CLOSED: §19.1 defines escrow behaviour, §38.6b defines the CommerceTransactionRule
engine and lifecycle, and §25/§25.2 already reference 'billing_management',
'invoice_history', and 'payout_details' as settings entries — but none of those are backed
by an actual schema, and no page was ever specified for a user to see their own order
history, download an invoice, manage a saved payment method, or see their subscription's
renewal date. This section is that schema layer and that page, sitting entirely on top of
existing rules — it introduces no new commission logic, no new escrow behaviour, and no
second checkout system.
```

```typescript
// ORDER — the concrete purchase record. One Order per completed §38.6b
// COMMERCE_TRANSACTION_LIFECYCLE run; a Marketplace cart (§19) may produce multiple Orders
// if items ship or fulfil independently, but never a second Order for the same lifecycle run.
interface Order {
  id:               string;                  // unique, server-generated — never client-supplied
  buyerId:          string;                   // PID or OPID
  sellerId:         string;                   // PID or OPID — the CommerceTransactionRule
                                                // (§38.6b) sellerClass applies here unchanged
  items: {
    listingId:      string;                    // MarketplaceListing (§19) or ProfessionalServiceListing (§38.1) id
    itemType:       'physical_product' | 'digital_product' | 'service' | 'template' | 'subscription';
    quantity:       number;
    unitPrice:      number;
    currency:       string;
  }[];
  subtotal:         number;
  commission:       number;                    // computed server-side only, per §38.6b — never
                                                // client-supplied, never editable after checkout
  tax:              number;
  total:            number;
  status:           'pending_payment' | 'paid' | 'in_escrow' | 'fulfilled'
                   | 'completed' | 'refunded' | 'partially_refunded' | 'disputed' | 'cancelled';
  escrowRef?:        string;                    // links to §19.1 ESCROW state when applicable
  idempotencyKey:   string;                    // §37.20 — a retried checkout with the same key
                                                // returns this same Order, never a duplicate
  placedAt:         Date;
  updatedAt:        Date;
  history:          StatusTransition[];        // reuses §4.9's existing StatusTransition type
}

// INVOICE — the document a buyer or institution downloads/receives. One Invoice per Order,
// or one Invoice per billing cycle for a Subscription (below) — never a duplicate for the
// same Order or the same cycle, enforced by the same idempotency discipline as §37.20.
interface Invoice {
  id:               string;
  orderId?:         string;                    // present for one-off purchases
  subscriptionId?:  string;                    // present for recurring billing cycles
  issuedTo:         string;                     // PID or OPID
  issuedAt:         Date;
  lineItems:        { description: string; amount: number }[];
  subtotal:         number; tax: number; total: number; currency: string;
  status:           'issued' | 'paid' | 'overdue' | 'void';
  downloadFormats:  ('pdf')[];                  // reuses §50.3's export-format discipline
}

// PAYMENT METHOD — a saved, tokenised instrument. PROFILE never stores raw card or bank
// details itself; only a processor-issued token, consistent with §27.2's encryption rules
// and §37.19's rule that financial truth is read from the provider's verified state, never
// reconstructed from client input.
interface PaymentMethod {
  id:               string;
  ownerId:          string;                     // PID or OPID
  type:             'card' | 'bank_account' | 'digital_wallet' | 'mobile_money';
  processorToken:   string;                     // opaque reference to the payment processor's
                                                 // own stored instrument — never a raw PAN/account number
  displayLabel:     string;                      // e.g. "Visa ···· 4242" — last four digits only
  isDefault:        boolean;
  addedAt:          Date;
  expiresAt?:       Date;
}

// SUBSCRIPTION — the individual or organisation's active plan (§32.1/§32.2), now with an
// actual record instead of only a plan name on the User/Organisation object.
interface Subscription {
  id:               string;
  ownerId:          string;                     // PID or OPID
  tier:             string;                      // one of §32.1's INDIVIDUAL_TIERS_V3 or
                                                 // §32.2's ORGANISATION_TIERS_V3 — same enums, not redefined
  billingCycle:     'monthly' | 'annual';
  status:           'active' | 'past_due' | 'cancelled' | 'trialing';
  currentPeriodEnd: Date;
  paymentMethodId:  string;                      // references PaymentMethod above
  cancelAtPeriodEnd: boolean;
}

const ORDER_PAYMENT_LIFECYCLE_INTEGRITY = [
  'an_Order_total_and_commission_are_always_read_from_the_server_record_never_recomputed_
   or_trusted_from_a_client_resubmission_(§37.17,_§37.20)',
  'a_PaymentMethod_display_label_never_exposes_a_full_card_or_account_number_anywhere_in_
   the_product_including_admin_tooling',
  'cancelling_a_Subscription_always_honours_the_remainder_of_the_current_paid_period_
   (restates §46.6\'s existing Terms of Service rule) before status_moves_to_cancelled',
  'every_Invoice_and_Order_is_visible_to_its_owner_indefinitely_regardless_of_subscription_
   status_—_billing_history_is_never_hidden_behind_an_active_paid_plan',
];
```

**User-facing surface:** a single **Orders & Billing** page, reachable from Settings
(§25/§25.2, wired below) and from the Marketplace (§19) purchase-history link, showing:
order history with status, downloadable invoices, saved payment methods, and current
subscription with renewal date and cancel/change-plan actions. One page, one schema set —
not a separate Marketplace-only order list and a separate Settings-only billing list.

```typescript
  chargeForVerification: boolean;          // OFF by default — verification is free for the requester
                                            // unless the institution explicitly activates this, same
                                            // toggle-only-activates-capability pattern as §38.6a.
  verificationFeeSchedule?: { verificationType: VerificationType; fee: number; currency: string }[];
                                            // priced per precise claim type (§4.4) — an institution can
                                            // charge for a full 'academic' degree confirmation while
                                            // keeping 'academic_affiliation' checks free, for instance.
}

// institution_sets_fee_if_any → user_pays_institution → institution_verifies → PROFILE_records_
// verification_and_commission_if_applicable) — this section only makes the OFF-by-default toggle
// and the fee schedule explicit; it does not replace that workflow. The requester always sees the
// full fee before paying (§38.6a's disclosure principle), and PROFILE's share is computed through
// the same CommerceTransactionRule engine (§38.6b), transactionType = 'institutional_verification' —
// not a seventh, separate commission calculation.
```

### 38.19 Revenue Stream Matrix (extends §32.3)

| Stream | Payer | Ecosystem Loop Stage |
|---|---|---|
| Individual subscriptions | Individuals | Identity / Discovery |
| Organisation subscriptions | Organisations | Discovery / Opportunity |
| Institution subscriptions | Institutions | Verification |
| Government partnerships | Governments | Verification / Trust |
| API licensing | Institutions, banks, employers | Verification |
| Verification transactions | Institutions | Verification |
| Recruitment services | Recruiters, Recruitment Partners | Opportunity |
| Marketplace commissions | Sellers | Commerce |
| Advertising | Verified individuals/orgs | Discovery |
| Service promotions | Individuals, organisations | Discovery |
| Premium templates | Creators | Commerce |
| Digital credential issuance | Institutions | Verification |
| Learning platforms | Organisations | Evidence |
| Enterprise infrastructure | Enterprises | Trust |
| Escrow & payments | All transacting parties | Commerce |
| Sponsored opportunities | Organisations | Opportunity |

```
INVESTOR NOTE: this diversified model reduces dependence on any single revenue
source and increases resilience as the platform scales — a deliberate design
outcome of the ecosystem loop, not a bolt-on monetization strategy.
```

### 38.20 Creator Commerce

A focused commerce layer for creative and intellectual output, deliberately scoped
**narrower** than the general Marketplace above rather than folded into it — Creator
Commerce is what a person *makes*; §38.4 Marketplace Evolution is what any account
*sells or offers as a service*. Keeping these distinct prevents the two from becoming
duplicate, competing systems.

```typescript
const CREATOR_COMMERCE_LISTING_TYPES = [
  'ebooks', 'courses', 'templates', 'digital_assets', 'software', 'photography',
  'research', 'consulting', 'event_tickets', 'memberships', 'workshops',
];

interface CreatorCommerceListing {
  id: string;
  creatorId: string;
  proofOfCreationId: string;        // links to §11.4 Copyright ID — every sale carries
                                      // verifiable provenance, not just a price tag
  listingType: typeof CREATOR_COMMERCE_LISTING_TYPES[number];
  linkedCreatorHubProfile: string;   // see §47.13 Creator Hub — this is not a second,
                                      // separate storefront system
}
```

Revenue split and escrow follow the same rules already defined in §11.4 (90/10
creator/platform) and §38.6 (PROFILE Payments) — no new payment rail is introduced.

# 39. FOUR-SIDED MARKETPLACE & VERIFIED HIRING NETWORK

### 39.0 Positioning Directive

```
PROFILE DOES NOT REPLACE RECRUITERS OR RECRUITMENT AGENCIES.

PROFILE becomes the trust layer connecting every participant in hiring:

Employer ↔ Recruitment Agency ↔ Recruiter ↔ PROFILE Trust Infrastructure
         ↔ Candidate ↔ Institutions ↔ Credential Authorities

This is the structural difference from LinkedIn and Handshake: PROFILE strengthens
every participant in the chain rather than disintermediating them.
```

### 39.1 The Four Participant Groups

| Group | Who | What They Want From PROFILE |
|---|---|---|
| **Professionals** | Individuals seeking jobs, contracts, collaborations, scholarships, freelance work | Discovery through evidence, not visibility |
| **Employers** | Companies, governments, NGOs, universities, startups, institutions | Lower time-to-hire, lower cost-per-hire, fewer bad hires, fraud reduction |
| **Recruitment Partners** | Agencies, executive search firms, staffing companies, talent consultants | Speed, candidate quality, successful placements, provable value |
| **Credential Authorities** | Universities, licensing bodies, certification bodies, regulators, employers | Scalable, low-friction verification |

Each group strengthens the others — this is the network effect underpinning §39.11 (VHN).

### 39.2 Recruitment Partner Account Type (extends §4.2 Organisation Schema)

```typescript
interface RecruitmentPartnerAccount extends Organisation {
  orgType: 'recruitment_partner';
  agencyBranding:      AgencyBrandingProfile;
  recruiterTeam:        RecruiterProfile[];
  candidatePipelines:   KanbanPipeline[];
  clientOrganisations:  string[];        // OPIDs of employer clients
  talentPools:          TalentPool[];
  placementAnalytics:   PlacementMetrics;
  trustRating:          TrustScore;      // same architecture as §13
  revenueTracking:      RevenueLedger;
}

interface RecruiterProfile {
  userId:       string;                  // PID of the individual recruiter
  agencyId:     string;
  role:         'recruiter' | 'lead_recruiter' | 'agency_admin';
  placements:   PlacementRecord[];
  reputationScore: number;               // derived from successful placements, not popularity
}
```

Recruiter workspace capabilities:

```typescript

];
// All actions happen inside PROFILE — no handoff to external tools required.
```

### 39.9 Talent Representation

```typescript
interface TalentRepresentation {
  candidateId:      string;               // PID
  representedBy:    string;               // Recruitment Partner OPID
  displayLabel:     string;                // e.g. "Represented by ABC Talent Partners"
  scope: {
    canNegotiateOnBehalf: boolean;
    canSubmitCandidate:    boolean;
    canManageOpportunities: boolean;
  };
  revocableByCandidate: true;              // always — no exceptions
}
// This is a uniquely-PROFILE feature: the sports-agent / talent-agent model
// applied to professional hiring, fully transparent and candidate-controlled.
```

### 39.10 Verified Hiring Network (VHN)

```
PROFILE       = the platform people use.
PROFILE OS    = the technology powering identity, trust, verification, and hiring.
VHN           = the specialised trust infrastructure for recruitment and employment,
                connecting employers, recruitment partners, recruiters, universities,
                certification bodies, and regulators.

Every hiring action — job posting, candidate verification, interview, offer,
employment confirmation, reference — flows through VHN, increasing trust and
reducing fraud as the network scales.
```

### 39.11 The PROFILE Hiring Loop

```
1.  User builds their PROFILE.
2.  User uploads evidence and credentials.
3.  Institutions verify them (VXI, §16).
4.  Trust Score grows (§13).
5.  Portfolio expands through recorded activity (§11).
6.  Recruiters and Recruitment Partners discover them (§39.4).
7.  Employers shortlist them (§39.3).
8.  Interviews happen inside PROFILE Messaging (§20).
9.  Offers are negotiated inside PROFILE Messaging.
10. The user is hired.
11. The employer verifies the employment (§16.2 Employment Verification).
12. The user's professional history updates (Work Evidence → Activity).
13. New skills and achievements are added.
14. The user becomes more discoverable — loop restarts at step 5.
```

### 39.12 Revenue Mapping by Participant

| Participant | Revenue Mechanism |
|---|---|
| PROFILE | Employer subscriptions, Recruitment Partner subscriptions, API licensing, featured listings, promoted agencies, advanced analytics, verification services, enterprise integrations |
| Recruitment Agencies | Placement fees, retained search, executive recruitment, talent consulting, subscription talent pools |
| Individual Recruiters | Verified recruiter profiles, reputation growth, placement portfolio, higher visibility |
| Professionals | Employment, freelance work, consulting, collaborations, scholarships, research opportunities, paid speaking, marketplace services |

### 39.13 Direct Connect — Freelancer-Initiated Business Discovery (extends §39.1's
Professionals ↔ Employers relationship, §39.8's consent-based introduction philosophy in
reverse, §17 Smart Search, §4.2 Organisation — not a scraped directory, not a new engine)

```
The problem this solves is real: freelancers on traditional platforms compete with hundreds
of bidders for one posting, spend hours writing proposals that go nowhere, get pushed into
racing rates to the bottom, and repeat the cycle daily with nothing compounding. The fix is
not a database of contact details scraped from businesses who never agreed to be found —
that solves the freelancer's problem by creating a worse one for everybody else, including
PROFILE itself (§27's abuse and fraud monitoring would have to treat PROFILE's own export
feature as an attack vector). The fix is flipping who initiates, inside the consent
boundary PROFILE already requires everywhere else in this document.

§39.8 already replaces cold email outreach with a structured, consent-based introduction
flow for the employer-to-candidate direction. Direct Connect is the same philosophy, run
in reverse: a verified professional can search and pitch verified businesses that have
explicitly opted in to being found — never a business that hasn't.
```

```typescript
interface DirectPitchPreferences {
  categories:          string[];        // service categories this business is open to hearing about
  budgetRange?:         { min: number; max: number; currency: string };
  minimumTrustTier?:    TrustTier;       // §13.1 — e.g. a business can require 'established' or above,
                                          // filtering out unverified accounts before a pitch ever lands
  responseCommitment?:  string;          // e.g. "within 3 business days" — sets expectations up front
}

// Discovery reuses §17.3 Global Smart Search exactly — filtered to organisations where
// openToDirectPitches = true — not a second search index:
const DIRECT_CONNECT_SEARCH_EXAMPLE =
  "gyms in London open to freelance pitches" -> §17.3 SMART_SEARCH, scoped by orgType + city + openToDirectPitches;

interface DirectPitch {
  id:              string;
  fromProfessionalId: string;
  toOrganisationId:   string;
  message:         string;              // drafted with PROFILE Advisor's help (§50.9.2) from the
                                         // sender's own verified evidence — never invented qualifications,
                                         // per §48.0's inference-vs-fact rule
  attachedEvidence: { trustScore: number; relevantActivities: string[]; portfolioLink: string };
                                         // the business sees a verified track record immediately,
                                         // not a cold stranger — this is what directly answers
                                         // problem #4 (poor client expectations, negative reviews)
  conversationType: 'consulting';       // routes through §20's messaging exactly as any other
                                         // conversation — no separate inbox, no exported contact data
  status:          'sent' | 'viewed' | 'responded' | 'declined';
  createdAt:       Date;
}
```

```typescript
const DIRECT_CONNECT_INTEGRITY_RULES = [
  'no_bulk_contact_export_ever_—_outreach_happens_through_PROFILE_messaging_(§20)_only,_
   so_every_pitch_stays_inside_PROFILE_audit_trail_abuse_reporting_and_moderation_(§28)',
  'a_business_only_appears_in_Direct_Connect_search_results_if_openToDirectPitches_=_true_—_
   set_by_that_business_itself_never_inferred_scraped_or_defaulted_on',
  'per-professional_pitch_rate_limits_apply_(§32.1_tier-based)_to_prevent_Direct_Connect_
   itself_from_becoming_a_spam_vector_inside_the_opted-in_pool',
  'a_business_receiving_repeated_low-quality_pitches_can_report_them_through_the_existing_
   Enforcement_Escalation_ladder_(§28.4)_—_not_a_new_moderation_system',
];
```

**Why this solves the original five problems, directly:**

1. *Competing with hundreds of freelancers* — structurally impossible here; there is no shared posting to bid on, only one-to-one outreach initiated by the professional.
2. *Hours writing proposals* — PROFILE Advisor (§50.9.2, free) drafts the pitch from verified evidence already on file.
3. *Racing rates to the bottom* — no visible competing bids exist for a business to compare against; rate is negotiated privately, one relationship at a time.
4. *Poor client expectations, negative reviews* — the business sees verified Trust Score and real evidence before the first reply, not a claim.
5. *Repeating the cycle daily with no improvement* — a successful Direct Connect engagement can become verified Activity and, if formalized through §38.6 PROFILE Commerce, a `CommerceEvidenceConsent` record (§38.6c) — compounding into the professional's evidence base rather than resetting to zero the next day.

**No commission on the resulting relationship.** PROFILE isn't a party to what a freelancer
and a business agree to off the back of a Direct Connect introduction — consistent with
§38.6b's principle that PROFILE earns when it enables, processes, protects, or completes a
transaction, not simply because two verified parties found each other. Search access itself
is a §32.1 plan feature (free tier gets a limited number of pitches per month; Professional
gets materially more); if the resulting engagement is later run through PROFILE Commerce for
escrow, invoicing, or dispute protection, §38.6b's normal commission applies to *that*, exactly
as it would for any other transaction — never retroactively to the introduction itself.

**Growth note (operational, not architectural):** the "1,000 gyms in London" outcome the
original idea wanted is achievable honestly — PROFILE actively inviting local businesses
(gyms, restaurants, law firms, and similar) to claim a free verified listing and opt in to
Direct Connect produces the same discoverable density, except every one of those thousand
gyms actually asked to be found.

---

# 40. ECOSYSTEM INTELLIGENCE, TRUST DISCOVERY & OPPORTUNITY ENGINE

### 40.0 Core Ecosystem Rule

```typescript
// Every action inside PROFILE must strengthen at least one of:
const ECOSYSTEM_VALUE_TARGETS = [
  'Identity', 'Trust', 'Verification', 'Discovery', 'Opportunity', 'Economic Growth',
];
// Nothing exists solely for engagement. PROFILE rewards contribution, evidence,
// competence, authenticity, and verification — never popularity, never vanity metrics.
// This restates and reinforces the Non-Negotiable Design Constraints in the Master Prompt preamble.
```

### 40.1 Professional Activity Layer Refinement (extends §11, §4.3)

```typescript
// PROFILE replaces social posting with Professional Contribution Infrastructure.
const ACTIVITY_ATTRIBUTION_FIELDS_V3 = [
  'skills_used', 'tools_used', 'institutions_involved', 'organisations_involved',
  'collaborators', 'clients', 'supervisors', 'mentors', 'research_partners',
];
```

### 40.2 Tool Attribution Engine (extends §4.8)

```typescript
// On tool tag, four automatic actions fire (unchanged from §4.8), reaffirmed here:
const TOOL_TAG_EFFECTS = [
  'add_to_user_tool_stack', 'add_to_cv', 'add_to_portfolio',
  'improve_discoverability', 'generate_tool_analytics',
];
// Tool providers may receive attribution signals only under an explicit
// commercial/legal agreement (see Master Prompt Open Item #5).
```

### 40.3 Activity → Evidence Pipeline (extends §5.2 Linkage Table)

```typescript
interface ActivityEvidenceToggles {
  addToCV:              boolean;
  addToPortfolio:        boolean;
  addToTrustEvidence:    boolean;
  requestVerification:   boolean;
}
// Users choose how each piece of evidence is used. Evidence becomes reusable
// across CV, Portfolio, Trust Center, and Opportunity applications via shared evidence IDs.
```

### 40.4 Discovery Engine Evolution (reaffirms §17.1, §17.2)

```
HARD RULE (restated): Discovery ranking never includes follower count, like
count, view count, share count, or any derivative. Zero exceptions, zero
configuration flags — this applies to every new discovery category added below.
```

```typescript
const DISCOVERY_CATEGORIES_V3 = [
  'People', 'Organisations', 'Projects', 'Research', 'Portfolios', 'Credentials',
  'Opportunities', 'Institutions', 'Tools', 'Industries', 'Skills', 'Services', 'Communities',
];
```

### 40.5 PROFILE References — Verified Recommendation Engine

```
References on PROFILE are never uploaded documents taken on faith. A reference is a
specific kind of verifiable credential — it inherits CredentialDocument's (§4.7) existing
digital signature, QR code, public validation URL, audit log, version history, and
expiration machinery directly. Nothing about signing, verifying, or expiring a reference is
invented twice; a reference simply IS a CredentialDocument with reference-specific
structured content attached.
```

```typescript
type ReferenceCategory = 'employment' | 'academic' | 'professional' | 'character' | 'skills' | 'government';

type ReferenceRelationship =
  // employment
  | 'former_employer' | 'current_employer' | 'supervisor' | 'manager' | 'hr_department'
  | 'ceo' | 'director' | 'project_manager' | 'team_lead' | 'client'
  // academic
  | 'professor' | 'lecturer' | 'dean' | 'head_of_department' | 'academic_supervisor'
  | 'research_advisor' | 'institution' | 'project_coordinator'
  // professional
  | 'mentor' | 'coach' | 'trainer' | 'professional_association' | 'licensing_body'
  | 'industry_expert' | 'consultant'
  // character
  | 'community_leader' | 'religious_leader' | 'volunteer_coordinator' | 'ngo' | 'verified_referee'
  // skills
  | 'project_collaborator' | 'freelance_client' | 'product_owner' | 'technical_lead'
  | 'creative_director' | 'architect' | 'engineer' | 'designer' | 'research_partner'
  // government
  | 'public_agency' | 'military' | 'police' | 'civil_service' | 'licensing_authority' | 'professional_council';

type TestimonialVerificationLevel =
  | 'standard' | 'verified' | 'institution_verified' | 'employer_verified' | 'client_verified';

type ReferenceVisibility =
  | 'private' | 'recruiters_only' | 'specific_organisations' | 'specific_opportunity'
  | 'public' | 'only_after_permission' | 'hidden';
// Extends the one visibility philosophy already established at §15.4/§25.1/§9.7 for this
// specific content type — not a seventh parallel privacy model.

type RatingLevel = 'excellent' | 'very_good' | 'good' | 'satisfactory' | 'developing' | 'not_rated';
// HARD RULE: numeric/rating values feed Trust Score weighting (§13.2) server-side, but are
// never displayed publicly unless both referee and subject explicitly permit it — a public
// reference shows the narrative and verification badge, never a raw internal score.

interface StructuredEvaluation {
  relationshipDuration?: string;
  capacity?:              string;
  projectsWorkedOn?:      string[];
  skillsDemonstrated?:    Skill[];        // reuses §4.3's controlled Skill taxonomy — not free text
  ratings?: {
    leadership?: RatingLevel; communication?: RatingLevel; technicalAbility?: RatingLevel;
    professionalEthics?: RatingLevel; reliability?: RatingLevel; innovation?: RatingLevel;
    teamwork?: RatingLevel; criticalThinking?: RatingLevel; problemSolving?: RatingLevel;
    attendance?: RatingLevel; integrity?: RatingLevel;
  };                                       // every field optional — a referee is never forced
                                            // to rate a dimension they have no basis to judge
  overallRecommendation?: RatingLevel;
  overallConfidence?:     RatingLevel;
  wouldRehire?:            boolean;
  wouldRecommend?:         boolean;
  narrative?:              string;         // structure and free text coexist, not either/or
}

interface ProfessionalReference extends CredentialDocument {
  // inherits verificationId, verificationSignature, verificationQR, publicValidationUrl,
  // auditLog, history, expiresAt, renewalReminder, trustWeight, badgeIds from §4.7 directly
  category:           ReferenceCategory;
  relationship:        ReferenceRelationship;
  refereeId:           string;              // referring person's PID, or an OPID if the
                                             // reference is issued on behalf of a verified org
  refereeVerificationLevel: TestimonialVerificationLevel;
  subjectId:           string;              // the person being referenced
  linkedEvidence:      { activityId?: string; employmentAffiliationId?: string; projectId?: string };
                                             // Hard Rule (restates §50.1 Risk #8): a reference
                                             // must link to something real — an unlinked
                                             // recommendation cannot exist on PROFILE
  structuredEvaluation: StructuredEvaluation;
  purpose?:  'employment' | 'scholarship' | 'promotion' | 'immigration' | 'recruitment'
           | 'university' | 'professional_registration' | 'grant';
  visibility:          ReferenceVisibility;
  status:              'requested' | 'pending' | 'completed' | 'declined' | 'expired' | 'withdrawn' | 'superseded';
}

// Three previously-referenced-but-undefined types (§4.6, §38.1), now closed:
interface ReviewEntry {          // a lighter, transaction-linked star rating — NOT a full
                                  // structured reference; used by Marketplace (§38.1, §38.6c)
  id: string; reviewerId: string; subjectId: string;
  transactionId?: string;        // links to §38.6c's CommerceEvidenceConsent where applicable
  rating: 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5;
  comment?: string;
  verified: boolean;             // true only when tied to a confirmed completed transaction
  createdAt: Date;
}
interface TestimonialEntry {     // lighter than ProfessionalReference — an endorsement with
                                  // a trust-weighted verification level but no structured evaluation
  id: string; authorId: string; subjectId: string;
  verificationLevel: TestimonialVerificationLevel;
  text: string; createdAt: Date;
}
type ReferenceEntry = ProfessionalReference;   // §47.2's CV "references" section renders
                                                 // ProfessionalReference records directly —
                                                 // not a fourth, separate shape

const TRUST_WEIGHTING_EXAMPLES = [
  { source: 'Verified Employer Recommendation',   weight: 'higher' },
  { source: 'Verified University Recommendation', weight: 'higher' },
  { source: 'Government/Licensing Authority Reference', weight: 'highest' },
  { source: 'Anonymous or Unverified Recommendation', weight: 'lower' },
];
// All weighting calculations must be transparent and traceable to a formula
// version, consistent with §13.3 Transparency Rules.
```

### 40.5.1 Request Workflow (extends §11.3 SelfTagRequest's existing accept/reject shape)

```typescript
interface ReferenceRequest {
  id:              string;
  requesterId:     string;       // the person requesting a reference about themselves,
                                  // OR a recruiter/organisation acting under
                                  // requestedOnBehalfOf below
  requestedOnBehalfOf?: { subjectId: string; requestedByOrgId: string };
                                  // NEW — a recruiter or employer (§18.3, §39.3) may request
                                  // a reference directly, but ONLY about a candidate who has
                                  // already applied to or engaged with them (§4.9 Application
                                  // record must exist) — never a cold request about a stranger.
                                  // The subject (subjectId) must explicitly approve before the
                                  // request reaches the referee, same consent gate §39.8's
                                  // Intelligent Introductions already requires for the reverse
                                  // direction — this is not a bypass of that principle.
  refereeId:       string;
  relationship:    ReferenceRelationship;
  linkedEvidence:  { activityId?: string; employmentAffiliationId?: string; projectId?: string };
  purpose?:        string;
  status:          'sent' | 'viewed' | 'accepted' | 'declined' | 'expired'
                  | 'pending_subject_approval';   // NEW — the recruiter-initiated request's
                                                   // holding state until the subject approves
  createdAt:       Date;
}
// Delivered and responded to through §20 Messaging exactly like §39.13's DirectPitch and
// §42.3's affiliation accept/reject — no separate reference-request inbox is built.
```

### 40.5.2 Referee Dashboard

```
Pending Requests, Completed References, Expired References, Declined Requests, Reference
History, and Frequently Referenced Users are filtered views over ProfessionalReference and
ReferenceRequest records already defined above — the same query-over-existing-records
pattern §18's Kanban application pipeline already uses. No new schema.
```

### 40.5.3 Letter Generation & Direct Delivery

```
A ProfessionalReference renders as a formatted PDF using the same export pipeline already
defined for CVs (§15.3's pdfDownload), with organisation branding pulled from the referee's
own Organisation profile (§4.2 logo/displayName) where they act on behalf of a verified org
— no second document-generation system. Delivery to an employer, university, government
body, or recruitment agency happens through §20 Messaging (conversationType 'verification',
already defined) or, for institution-to-institution delivery, the same channel §16's VXI
uses to route verification requests — never a new delivery mechanism, and never a manually
emailed attachment as the primary path.
```

### 40.5.4 AI-Assisted Referee Support (extends §48.1 AI Career Assistant, bound by §48.0)

```
A referee drafting the narrative portion of StructuredEvaluation gets the same AI Career
Assistant support already defined at §48.1 — wording suggestions, professionalism and
readability improvement, bias and discriminatory-language detection. §48.0's existing Hard
Rule governs without exception: AI may improve how something is said, never invent what was
demonstrated. Structured ratings are populated only by the referee's own input — AI never
auto-fills a rating field.
```

### 40.5.5 Reference Template Library

```typescript
const REFERENCE_TEMPLATE_LIBRARY = [
  'Employment Recommendation', 'Academic Recommendation', 'Internship Evaluation',
  'Research Recommendation', 'Graduate School Recommendation', 'Scholarship Recommendation',
  'Volunteer Recommendation', 'Professional Membership Recommendation', 'Promotion Recommendation',
  'Immigration Recommendation', 'Government Recommendation', 'Reference Confirmation',
  'Character Reference', 'Project Completion Letter', 'Consultancy Reference',
  'Freelancer Recommendation', 'Trade Skill Recommendation', 'Apprenticeship Recommendation',
  'Mentorship Evaluation', 'Executive Recommendation',
];
// Same template-library pattern as §47.5 CV_TEMPLATE_LIBRARY / §47.6 PORTFOLIO_TEMPLATE_LIBRARY
// — twenty content variations on the one ProfessionalReference schema above, not twenty schemas.
```

### 40.5.6 Fraud Prevention & Trust Integration (cross-references only — both already exist)

```
Fake referees: closed by §50.1's existing ReferenceInvitation (Risk #7) — a referee must
verify their own identity before any reference is accepted, no exception. Self-references
and unlinked recommendations: closed by §50.1's Risk #8 rule, now structural via
ProfessionalReference.linkedEvidence above — a reference with no linked evidence cannot be
created, not merely discouraged by policy. Circular reference rings and mass fake
endorsements: already covered by §50.1's circular_endorsement_or_employment_cluster and
bot_account_signals risk signals — nothing new required. Trust Score contribution: already
governed by §13.2's peer_endorsement_higher_tier weight, refined above by
TRUST_WEIGHTING_EXAMPLES to reflect that government/licensing-authority references carry
more weight than an unverified personal reference — one formula, versioned per §13.3, not a
second scoring system for references specifically.
```

# 41. PLATFORM POSITIONING & INFRASTRUCTURE ADDENDUM

### 41.1 Domain Architecture — Resolved (see §50.8)

```
Master Prompt v2.0 originally proposed: profileos.com · getprofileos.com · myprofileos.com ·
                                          profiletrust.com · builtonproof.com

STATUS: RESOLVED at §50.8 (Product Positioning Resolution & Final Validation Gate).
Consumer Platform = "PROFILE" = www.myprofile.com. Technology Platform = "PROFILE OS" =
www.profileos.com. Legacy domains above redirect into these two canonical domains.
This entry is retained for historical traceability only — §50.8 is the binding source.
```

### 41.2 The PROFILE Difference

| Traditional Platforms Ask | PROFILE Asks |
|---|---|
| "Who says you are qualified?" | "Can it be verified?" |
| "How many followers do you have?" | "What evidence supports your claims?" |
| Rewards popularity | Rewards authenticity, verification, contribution, trust |

### 41.3 Core Constituencies (reaffirms Master Prompt §1 audience)

```typescript
const CORE_CONSTITUENCIES = {
```

### 41.4 Investor Thesis — Network Effects

```
Every new user increases institutional value.
Every new institution increases user value.
Every new verification increases trust value.

This trust network compounds and becomes increasingly difficult to replicate.
The Verification Exchange Infrastructure (VXI, §16) is the most defensible
asset in the ecosystem — it is the layer competitors would need to rebuild
relationship-by-relationship with every university, employer, and regulator.
```

### 41.5 Founder's Thesis

```
The future economy increasingly relies on trust, evidence, and verifiable
contribution. Professional identity, credentials, work history, and
opportunity remain fragmented across disconnected systems today.

PROFILE exists to unify these systems into a single operating layer for
professional trust — not by replacing any single participant in the
ecosystem, but by making every participant more effective within it.
```

# 42. ORGANISATION TEAM, ROLES & PERMISSIONS

### 42.0 Gap Identified (closed)

Master Prompt §4.2 originally defined `adminRoles: OrgAdminRole[]` (max 20 admins) with a narrow
functional role set (`super_admin`, `verification_officer`, `hr_manager`, `credential_manager`,
`moderator`, `compliance_officer`). What was missing — and what this section adds — is a generic
`admin` tier, an explicit permission matrix, an invite/accept/revoke workflow, an ownership rule,
and the Settings UI surface to manage it. Nothing in §4.2 is removed; this extends it. As of the
§4.2's `adminRoles` field is retyped to `OrgAdminAssignment[]` (using the
`OrgAdminRoleV3` union defined next) so the schema field and this section's operational logic
agree — closing the type-drift gap the original  audit flagged.

### 42.1 Extended Role Set

```typescript
// EXTENDS §4.2 Organisation Schema — adminRoles field
type OrgAdminRoleV3 =
  | 'super_admin'          // full control incl. billing; sole role that can appoint/remove any other role
  | 'admin'                // general management; cannot touch billing or remove super_admin
  | 'verification_officer' // reviews and decides on incoming credential verification requests
  | 'hr_manager'           // manages job postings, hiring pipeline, staff records
  | 'credential_manager'   // issues and manages badges/credentials on behalf of the org
  | 'moderator'            // handles reported content and community standards in org spaces
  | 'compliance_officer';  // audit log access, legal holds, data subject requests

interface OrgAdminAssignment {
  userId:      string;                  // PID of the assigned individual
  orgId:       string;                  // OPID
  role:        OrgAdminRoleV3;
  assignedBy:  string;                  // PID of the super_admin/admin who granted it
  assignedAt:  Date;
  status:      'invited' | 'active' | 'revoked';
  auditTrail:  SecurityAuditEntry[];    // ties into §37.7
}
```

### 42.2 Permission Matrix

| Capability | super_admin | admin | verification_officer | hr_manager | credential_manager | moderator | compliance_officer |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Billing & subscription | ✓ | – | – | – | – | – | – |
| Appoint / remove admin roles | ✓ | limited* | – | – | – | – | – |
| Edit org profile & portfolio | ✓ | ✓ | – | – | – | – | – |
| Review verification requests | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | – | – | – | – |
| Issue credentials/badges | ✓ | ✓ | – | – | ✓ | – | – |
| Post jobs / manage hiring pipeline | ✓ | ✓ | – | ✓ | – | – | – |
| Moderate org community content | ✓ | ✓ | – | – | – | ✓ | – |
| View audit logs | ✓ | ✓ | own actions only | own actions only | own actions only | own actions only | ✓ |
| Handle legal holds / data requests | ✓ | – | – | – | – | – | ✓ |

*`admin` may invite/remove any role except `super_admin`.*

### 42.3 Ownership & Invitation Rules

```
RULE 1 — SOLE OWNERSHIP NEVER ORPHANED
Every organisation account must have exactly one active super_admin at all times.
A super_admin may not remove themselves without first transferring the role.

RULE 2 — INVITE BEFORE ACTIVE
A role assignment is created with status = 'invited' and carries zero permissions
until the invited user accepts inside PROFILE. Invites expire after 7 days.

RULE 3 — CAP PRESERVED
The existing 20-admin cap from §4.2 is preserved across ALL roles combined,
including the new generic 'admin' tier.

RULE 4 — RECOVERY PATH
If the sole super_admin is unreachable, recovery follows the Compliance Officer
escalation path (documented, identity-verified, admin-assisted — same rigor as
§7.3 Path 4 Identity Recovery for individual accounts).

RULE 5 — EVERY CHANGE IS AUDITED
Every grant, acceptance, and revocation is written to SecurityAuditEntry (§37.7).

RULE 6 — ADMIN ROLES REQUIRE PRIOR AFFILIATION
A user may only be invited into an OrgAdminRole (§42.1) if they already hold an 'active'
OrgStaffRecord (§4.2) with that organisation. Organisations do not create standalone admin
accounts disconnected from an existing staff/member relationship — they promote someone
already in their People roster. Rules 1–5 above are otherwise unchanged.
```

### 42.3.1 Ordinary Affiliations Use the Same Accept-Before-Active Pattern

Rule 2 above (invite-before-active) already governs `OrgAdminRole` invitations. The same
pattern applies to ordinary `OrgStaffRecord` affiliations (§4.2) — employment, faculty,
student, volunteer, contractor, board member, etc. — which is the prerequisite Rule 6 above
depends on:

```
RULE — AFFILIATION REQUIRES CONFIRMATION
A new OrgStaffRecord is created with status = 'invited' and does not appear as Verified
anywhere (profile, staff directory, Career Timeline §50.9.6) until the individual responds.
Options: Accept (→ 'active') / Reject (→ removed) / Request Changes (→ returns to the
organisation for edit, remains 'invited'). Once accepted, the affiliation renders on:
Individual Profile (§24.1 Organizations & Roles), Organisation Profile (§24.2 Staff),
Career Timeline (§50.9.6), Trust Center (§12), and Analytics (§23) — no duplicate data
created across these surfaces.

NOT-YET-REGISTERED PATH: an organisation may enter Name, Email, Position, Department for
someone without a PROFILE account. Status is 'pending_profile_registration'. On matching-
email registration, §8's existing onboarding identity-matching auto-links the affiliation —
no re-entry of data required by the organisation.
```

### 42.4 Settings UI Surface (extends §37.9 Settings Center)

```typescript
// New tab: Settings → Team (organisation accounts only)
const ORG_TEAM_TAB = {
  memberList: { showsRoleBadge: true, showsStatus: true, showsAssignedBy: true },
  inviteAction: { inputsUsernameOrEmail: true, roleDropdown: 'restricted_to_grantable_roles' },
  removeAction: { requiresConfirmation: true, blockedForSoleSuperAdmin: true },
  auditLink: 'links_to_compliance_officer_audit_view_if_permitted',
};
```

# 43. ABOUT, VISION, MISSION & PLATFORM NARRATIVE

### 43.0 Purpose

This is the canonical copy for the `/about` route and its sub-sections (extends §37.14's
section list with the actual written content, ready to route into the app).

### 43.1 Vision

> A world where every person, institution, organisation, and professional achievement can be
> trusted, verified, and discovered — where opportunity is earned through evidence and
> contribution rather than visibility alone, and where trust itself becomes a universal
> economic asset.

### 43.2 Mission

> To build the world's most trusted professional identity infrastructure — where work becomes
> credibility, credibility becomes opportunity, and opportunity creates sustainable economic
> growth for individuals, organisations, institutions, and governments alike.

### 43.3 Philosophy

> Most platforms ask "who says you're qualified?" and settle for whatever answer you type.
> PROFILE asks "can it be verified?" — and routes the question to the university, employer,
> or licensing body that actually knows. We don't rank people by how loud they are. We rank
> evidence by how well it holds up.

### 43.4 Why PROFILE Exists

```
The global professional ecosystem has a trust problem at every layer:
  Identity     → fake identities, impersonation, no universal record
  Credentials  → forged certificates, unverifiable degrees, fake licences
  Employment   → inflated CVs, unreliable reference checks
  Institutions → manual verification, bottlenecks, lost records
  Opportunity  → qualified people stay undiscovered; recruiters drown in noise
  Economy      → fraud costs, bad hires, underused talent

PROFILE exists to replace claims with confirmed evidence at every one of these layers.
```

### 43.5 Founder Story

> *[Founder-provided narrative to be inserted here by Omini Nfawa — recommended structure below.]*
> — What professional or personal experience surfaced the trust gap PROFILE solves
> — The moment the idea became a platform, not just a frustration
> — What "built on proof" means personally, not just as a tagline

### 43.6 Identity Infrastructure, Trust Model & Security Model — Summary Panels

```typescript
const ABOUT_PAGE_SUMMARY_PANELS = [
  { title: 'Identity Infrastructure', body: 'Every user has an immutable PID (§9.2). Names are never the identity — evidence is.' },
  { title: 'Trust Model',             body: 'Trust Score (§13) is built entirely from verified signals, weighted and versioned, never from followers or likes.' },
  { title: 'Security Model',          body: 'Encryption at rest and in transit, MFA, RLS on every table, full audit logging (§37).' },
  { title: 'Verification Exchange (VXI)', body: 'The routing layer connecting users to institutions, employers, and regulators (§16).' },
];
```

### 43.7 Use Cases By Constituency

| Constituency | What PROFILE Solves For Them |
|---|---|
| Individuals | A career record no one can fake, that updates itself as they work |
| Organisations | Faster hiring, verified staff, reduced fraud exposure |
| Universities | Automated alumni/degree verification at scale, lower admin cost |
| Governments | Credential transparency, workforce intelligence, fraud reduction |
| Enterprises | White-label trust infrastructure, private verification networks (§38.18) |

### 43.8 Economic Impact Statement

> Trust is expensive when it's manual. PROFILE makes verification fast enough that qualified
> people stop being invisible and institutions stop being verification bottlenecks — turning
> credential-checking from a cost center into infrastructure that pays for itself in reduced
> fraud and faster hiring.

### 43.9 Roadmap (Public-Facing, High-Level Only)

### 44.3 Voice In Practice

> *Most places ask you to prove yourself with a headline and a hope. PROFILE asks for
> something better: your actual work, confirmed by the people who saw it happen. Your
> university confirms the degree. Your employer confirms the role. Your clients confirm
> the outcome. None of it is claimed — all of it is checked. That's not a feature. That's
> the whole point of building something on proof.*
