### 50.9 Professional Growth Intelligence (PGI)

#### 50.9.0 Positioning

PGI is a structured coaching and assessment layer, not a chatbot — it continuously reads
data every other module already owns (activity, verification, trust, portfolio) and turns
it into role-aware, career-stage-aware guidance. It introduces no new data store; it is an
intelligence layer over §4 (Schema), §11 (Activity), §13 (Trust Score), §15 (CV Engine),
and §47 (Career Engine). This extends the philosophy first stated in §1.4 into: **Learning
→ Identity → Evidence → Verification → Trust → Opportunity → Growth → Economic Prosperity.**

#### 50.9.1 Career Stage (extends §4.1 User Schema)

`accountType` and `pathway` never change what a user *is*; `careerStage` (defined next to
`NameAlias` in §4.1) is what evolves as they grow — a user never migrates accounts to stop
"being a student." Historical career-stage milestones are preserved, not overwritten, on
every transition, feeding directly into the Trust Timeline (§50.5).

```typescript
interface CareerStageTransition {
  from: CareerStage; to: CareerStage; transitionedAt: Date;
  preservedMilestones: string[];   // refs into TrustTimelineEntry (§50.5) — never deleted
}
```

#### 50.9.2 PROFILE Advisor — the AI Career Assistant (extends §48.10 Personal AI
Professional Agent — same agent; operates as PROFILE Copilot's "Career Copilot" skill per
§48.0a's Copilot Skills Framework, under its own established, free-tier user-facing name)

```
PROFILE Advisor is the single, free, always-available AI agent every individual on PROFILE
has access to, regardless of plan — the same persistent agent defined at §48.10
(PersonalAIAgentContext, AI_AGENT_CAPABILITIES), not a second, narrower agent. It is not a
Section 23 analytics feature and is never gated by the Professional or Enterprise tier
(§32.1) — the deeper reporting on the Analytics page (profile view trends, network maps,
recruiter-interest history) is Premium; the agent that actively works on a person's behalf
is not, and must never become so.

What it actually does, in plain terms:
- Arranges and continuously re-arranges a person's CV and Portfolio (§15) as new verified
  activity arrives, choosing section order, emphasis, and template fit for their pathway —
  the person can always override any of it, but they never have to start from a blank page.
- Runs Smart Job Placement: matches a person against open Opportunity Hub (§18) listings
  using their verified skills, tagged tools, and demonstrated experience — not job titles
  alone — and surfaces the strongest matches first.
- Generates job recommendations ranked by fit against tools used, skills demonstrated, and
  verified experience (§4.8 Tool.industryAdoption, Skill taxonomy §4.3), so a self-taught
  editor with three strong verified projects is matched on the strength of that work, not
  excluded for lacking a big-name employer on file.
- Performs the Career Readiness Engine's Skill Gap Analysis and Opportunity Prediction
  (§50.9.4) — telling a person exactly what specific, achievable step would most improve
  their odds for a target role, never a bare score with no explanation.
```

```typescript
const PROFILE_ADVISOR_EVALUATES = [
  'identity_completeness', 'evidence_quality', 'verification_status', 'portfolio_quality',
  'cv_quality', 'skills_coverage', 'tool_coverage', 'recommendations', 'references',
  'professional_activity', 'recruiter_readiness', 'academic_readiness', 'business_readiness',
  'trust_growth', 'job_match_strength',
];
// Bound by §48.0 AI Engine Principles — recommendations cite the specific missing evidence
// (e.g. "Verify your highest qualification," "Tag collaborators on your last project"),
// never a bare percentage with no explanation. Updates dynamically as the profile evolves.
```

#### 50.9.3 Profile Health Dashboard (extends §23 Analytics)

```typescript
interface ProfileHealthScores {
  identityHealth: number; portfolioHealth: number; trustHealth: number; verificationHealth: number;
  recruiterReadiness: number; careerReadiness: number; academicReadiness: number;
  businessReadiness: number; marketplaceReadiness: number; opportunityReadiness: number;
}
// Each score ships with at least one concrete recommendation — never a number alone.
// Surfaces inside the existing Individual Analytics panel (§23.1); not a new analytics page.
```

#### 50.9.4 Career Readiness Engine & Skill Gap Analysis (extends §48 AI Engine, §33-equivalent skill tooling)

```
Permanently free (§32.1) — this is the smart job matching and recommendation capability
PROFILE Advisor performs on a person's behalf (§50.9.2); it is never a paywalled add-on.
```

```typescript
interface SkillGapAnalysis {
  targetRole: string;
  currentCompetencies: Skill[]; requiredCompetencies: Skill[];
  gaps: { skill: string; recommendedAction: 'certification' | 'project' | 'learning_resource' | 'mentor' }[];
}
// Recommendations may point to Learning & Communities (§21), Collab Hub (§22) for mentors,
// or Opportunity Hub (§18) — never a disconnected standalone recommendation list.

// Opportunity Prediction — extends SkillGapAnalysis with a probability
// estimate. Bound by §48.0 AI Transparency rule: always labeled as an estimate, never framed
// as a guarantee, and always paired with the specific gaps that would raise it.
interface OpportunityPrediction {
  targetRole: string;
  estimatedProbability: number;         // 0–100
  basis: ('verified_experience' | 'portfolio_evidence' | 'market_demand')[];
  gapClosingActions: string[];          // pulled directly from SkillGapAnalysis.gaps
  disclaimer: string;                   // required, e.g. "Estimate based on available signals, not a guarantee"
}
```

#### 50.9.5 Role-Specific Guidance (cross-references §8.3 individual categories + Career Stage)

| Career Stage Cluster | Emphasis | Governing Prompt |
|---|---|---|
| Learner / Student / Undergraduate | Academic projects, competitions, volunteer work, learning milestones | "What are you learning?" not "Where have you worked?" |
| Emerging Professional / Fresh Graduate / Intern | Final-year projects, internships, open-source, potential over tenure | Portfolio evidence over years of experience |
| Professional / Executive | Work evidence, verified credentials, references, career progression | Strengthen what's already built |
| Skilled Tradesperson | Before/after project media, equipment used, trade certifications, client testimonials | Evidence-first, same as §8.3's Competence category |
| Freelancer / Entrepreneur | Service catalogue, case studies, verified clients, proposal success rate | Ties into §38.20 Creator Commerce and §38 Marketplace |
| Researcher | Publications, grants, conferences, patents, collaborators, optional ORCID | Ties into §19 Copyright & Proof of Creation |
| Mentor / Retired Professional | Verified guidance given, mentorship history | Ties into §50.9.8 Mentorship |

This table routes guidance; it does not create separate pages per stage — every stage
renders through the same Profile, CV/Portfolio, and Advisor components with different
prompts and emphasis.

#### 50.9.5a Academic & Independent Sub-Stage Guidance (keyed by `journeyStage`, §8.8.1)

The table above routes on the broad `CareerStage` cluster. Within the "Learner / Student /
Undergraduate" and "Skilled Tradesperson" rows specifically, `journeyStage` (§4.1) adds the
finer granularity a first-year student and a final-year student both need, but need
differently — this table is the single canonical source for that emphasis; the checklist
items in §50.9.11 are this table's prompts made concrete, not a second, independently
maintained list.

| `journeyStage` | Emphasis | Governing Prompt |
|---|---|---|
| Foundation / Pre-university | Exploration, discovering interests, early skill-building | "What are you curious about?" |
| Year 1 | First recorded project, first collaborator tag, joining a society or competition | "What have you started?" |
| Year 2 / Year 3 | Growing project depth, breadth of skills, internship readiness | "What are you building on?" |
| Final Year | Capstone or thesis, internships, first verification requests | "What's ready to be verified?" |
| Master's — Year 1 | Research direction, coursework depth, advisor and lab relationships | "What question are you pursuing?" |
| Master's — Final Year | Thesis, publications-in-progress, defence preparation | "What's ready to defend?" |
| PhD — Early Stage | Research proposal, literature review, supervisor and research-partner tagging | "What's your contribution going to be?" |
| PhD — Final Stage | Publications, conference presentations, thesis defence, patents | "What have you proven?" |
| Postgraduate Diploma | Applied project work, professional certification alignment | "What can you now do that you couldn't before?" |
| Just starting out (independent) | First practice piece, tools and materials used (§4.8 Tool attribution) | "What did you make?" |
| Building a body of work (independent) | Portfolio depth, breadth of client-ready samples | "What's your strongest piece?" |
| Already taking on clients or selling (independent) | Verified client reviews, Store listings, repeat business | "Who can vouch for you?" |

`journeyStage` is optional and additive — a person who never went through the new §8.8.1
setup, or whose `careerStage` isn't academic or independent-craft, is routed by the broad
table above exactly as before; nothing here changes existing behaviour for them.

#### 50.9.6 Career Timeline (extends §50.5 Trust Timeline — not a duplicate)

The "Career Timeline" described in career-growth terms is the same chronological ledger as
the Trust Passport's Trust Timeline (§50.5), broadened to explicitly include education,
internships, competitions, promotions, business launches, and professional memberships as
`TrustTimelineEntry.eventType` values — not a second timeline data store.

**Professional Growth Journey — named route ():** promoted to
`/career-growth/journey`, a sub-route of the existing Career Growth page (`/career-growth`).
Renders the Career Timeline above alongside the Health Dashboards (§50.9.3) as a single
combined view of the Ecosystem Loop (§1.4) in motion — no new schema, no new top-level nav
entry; reached via a tab within Career Growth, not the Hamburger Menu (§10.4).

#### 50.9.7 PROFILE Academy (extends §21 Learning & Communities)

```typescript
const PROFILE_ACADEMY_TOPICS = [
  'building_a_professional_profile', 'creating_an_effective_portfolio', 'cv_and_cover_letter_guidance',
  'interview_preparation', 'career_planning', 'business_profile_optimisation',
  'research_presentation', 'trade_and_artisan_branding', 'personal_branding', 'networking_best_practices',
];
// Delivered through the existing LEARN_CONTENT_TYPES (§21.1) — self-paced courses, cohort
// workshops — adapted by Career Stage (§50.9.1), not a separate learning product.
```

#### 50.9.8 Mentorship & Verified Guidance (extends §21.2 Communities, §40.5 PROFILE References)

Mentors, lecturers, supervisors, employers, and clients may provide guidance and
recommendations that — when verified — contribute Trust Score weight exactly as
`peer_endorsement_higher_tier` already defines in §13.2. Mentorship relationships may be
public or private per the user's visibility settings (§25.1).

**Contribution Index:** a roll-up specifically for helping others,
surfaced on the profile separate from self-promotional content:

```typescript
interface ContributionScore {
  mentoringSessions: number;
  portfolioReviewsGiven: number;
  caseStudiesContributed: number;
  openResearchContributions: number;
  volunteeringVerified: number;
  rollupScore: number;                  // single number surfaced on profile
}
// Feeds TrustSignal (§13.2) using the existing peer_endorsement_higher_tier weight — no new
// trust category unless that weight proves insufficient at launch.
```

#### 50.9.9 Opportunity Matching Enhancement (extends §18 Opportunity Hub)

Matching already prioritises verified qualifications and skills (§18); this adds Career
Stage and Profile Health signals (§50.9.1, §50.9.3) as additional ranking inputs — still
governed by the same Hard Rule as §17.1: never popularity, never engagement.

#### 50.9.11 Strategic Growth Journey (extends §50.9.2 PROFILE Advisor, §50.9.6 Career
Timeline, §19 Marketplace, §8 Onboarding — not a new engine, not a new evidence schema)

```
The core insight: by the time most platforms meet a person, their evidence is already
scattered across four years of unrecorded work, or dismissed entirely because it was never
formal. This section makes day one — a first-year student's first week of Year 1, or a
self-taught craftsperson's first real piece of work — the actual starting line for their
PROFILE, not graduation or a first paying client.

This applies to two populations equally and by design, because §8.3's Competence category
carries the same weight as its Academic category everywhere in this document:

1. Students at every level — first-year through final-year Bachelor's, Poly/Diploma,
   Master's, PhD — recording coursework, projects, competitions, research assistantships,
   and society leadership as it happens, not reconstructed from memory at graduation.
2. Self-taught and informally-trained people with no enrolled institution — recording
   their craft as it develops: first practice pieces, first client work, first sale — with
   the same seriousness and the same visible path to a strong PROFILE.

Every milestone below is a normal Activity record (§4.3) tagged and dated as it happens.
No new schema is introduced; this section defines the onboarding prompts, checkpoint
copy, and Advisor nudges that make recording that Activity feel like the obvious, worthwhile
thing to do from day one — not a new mechanism for storing it.
```

**Journey Setup happens at onboarding — see §8.8.1 for the actual onboarding step
(`ACADEMIC_JOURNEY_SETUP` / `INDEPENDENT_JOURNEY_SETUP`).** It is placed there, not here,
because Section 8 is Registration & Onboarding's one home per §50.7's Feature Routing
Matrix; this section covers only what happens after setup — the checklists and delivery.

**Strategic Journey Checklists — delivered through the existing Daily Return Engine
(§11.0), not a new widget:**

```typescript
const STRATEGIC_JOURNEY_CHECKLIST: Record<string, string[]> = {
  // Each entry below is §50.9.5a's Governing Prompt for that journeyStage, made concrete
  // as 2-3 checkable actions. If the two ever disagree, §50.9.5a is correct — update this
  // table to match it, not the other way around.
  'Year 1 (Bachelor\u2019s / Poly / Diploma)': [
    'Record one class project or lab assignment worth keeping',
    'Join one society, club, or competition',
    'Tag a classmate as a collaborator on group work',
  ],
  'Final Year': [
    'Record your capstone or thesis project',
    'Record any internship or industrial attachment',
    'Request verification for your strongest piece of coursework',
  ],
  'PhD — Early Stage': [
    'Record your research proposal or literature review as a milestone',
    'Tag your supervisor and any research partners',
  ],
  'PhD — Final Stage': [
    'Record every publication, conference presentation, and patent as it happens',
    'Request institutional verification on your thesis defence',
  ],
  'Just starting out': [
    'Upload your first practice piece or sample of work',
    'Record what tools and materials you used — this feeds §4.8 Tool attribution',
  ],
  'Already taking on clients or selling': [
    'Record your first client project with a request for a verified review',
    'Publish your first Store listing (§19)',
  ],
  // Full table covers all values from §50.9.1's CareerStage union; abbreviated here for
  // brevity — every stage gets a checklist, none are left generic.
};

// Delivery: PROFILE Advisor (§50.9.2) surfaces the next unchecked item as one of its
// 2–4 Daily Return Engine recommendations, framed as an invitation, never a deficiency —
// e.g. "You're a Year 1 student — recording one project now is what most strong PROFILEs
// in your field already have by this point. Want to start yours?" Governed by the same
// AI Transparency rule as §48.0: always tied to a specific, achievable action, never a
// bare score or a guilt-based prompt. This is Non-Negotiable Design Constraint 2's
// "progress, not popularity" principle applied to students specifically — the challenge
// is to their own record, never a comparison to another named person.
```

#### 50.9.12 Institution Excellence Dashboard (extends §16.5 Institutional Review
Console — not a new verification engine)

```
The problem this closes: a university that verifies a hundred graduates a year currently
gets nothing back for it — no visibility into whether verification actually translated into
outcomes their next prospectus could point to. This gives institutions a reason to actively
champion PROFILE rather than merely tolerate it as a verification request queue.

Every metric below is aggregate by default and drills into individual detail only where
that individual has separately consented (reusing §25's existing visibility settings —
no new consent mechanism). An institution never sees a named student's private data because
it verified their degree; verification and visibility remain governed separately, exactly
as they already are in §16 and §25.
```

```typescript
interface InstitutionExcellenceDashboard {
  verifiedAlumniCount: number;
  aggregateTrustScoreGrowth: number;         // cohort average, anonymised
  verifiedOutcomes: {                         // each entry only from alumni who opted in to share it
    employmentPlacements: number;
    researchPublications: number;
    businessesLaunched: number;
    notableAchievements: { title: string; alumniConsented: true }[];
  };
  embeddableExcellenceWidget: string;         // reuses §16.7's existing embeddable trust
                                               // badge widget, at institution rather than
                                               // individual scope — for the institution's
                                               // own website or prospectus
}
// Surfaces inside the existing Institution Hub dashboard (§16, §8.4 hub routing) — not a
// new top-level module. The commercial logic is direct: an institution whose graduates
// visibly succeed has a standing reason to keep recommending PROFILE to its next intake.
```

#### 50.9.10a Named Growth Surfaces — Branding Aliases

Three terms — **Graduate Launchpad**, **Student Opportunity Hub**, and **Professional
Growth Journey** — were approved as product concepts but did not exist anywhere in the
document under those names. Per Patch Law 3 (Ecosystem-Aligned Only) and Rule 3 (No
Duplicate Modules), these are implemented as **named, branded views over existing
capabilities**, not new schemas or engines:

```
"Student Opportunity Hub" = §18.5 — routed to /opportunity/students. Pre-filters §18
  Opportunity Hub to scholarships, internships, fellowships, research grants, and
  entry-level roles, for users whose careerStage (§50.9.1) is in the 'learner' /
  'student' / 'undergraduate' / 'technical_vocational_learner' cluster (§50.9.5).
  No new data model — a routed, filtered view over §18's existing opportunity taxonomy.

"Graduate Launchpad" = §18.5 — routed to /opportunity/graduates. Same Opportunity Hub
  filtering, widened to the 'emerging_professional' / 'fresh_graduate' / 'job_seeker' /
  'intern' / 'national_service_participant' cluster (§50.9.5), combined on one landing
  surface with the Career Readiness Engine's Skill Gap Analysis and Opportunity
  Prediction output (§50.9.4). No new schema — a routed, composed view over §18 and §50.9.4.

"Professional Growth Journey" = §50.9.6 — routed to /career-growth/journey. The
  user-facing name for the Ecosystem Loop (§1.4: Identity → Evidence → Verification →
  Trust → Discovery → Opportunity → Growth) as experienced through the Career Timeline
  (§50.9.6) and Health Dashboards (§50.9.3). No new schema — a routed view over
  already-shipped surfaces.
```

**Resolution ():** all three are promoted to dedicated,
bookmarkable/shareable routes — but as sub-routes of their owning page (Opportunity Hub,
Career Growth), never as new bottom-nav tabs (§10.1) or Hamburger Menu entries (§10.4).
This keeps the Rule 4 navigation exception scoped exactly as originally authorized while
still giving each surface a real, linkable identity. See §18.5 and §50.9.6 for the route
definitions.

#### 50.9.10 Architecture Integrity Check

```
✓ No duplicate schemas — Career Timeline reuses TrustTimelineEntry (§50.5); PROFILE
  Academy reuses LEARN_CONTENT_TYPES (§21.1); Mentorship reuses the Verified
  Recommendation Engine (§40.5); PROFILE Advisor reuses AI Engine principles (§48.0)
✓ No new account types — Career Stage is a User field (§4.1), not a new schema
✓ No broken routes — PGI surfaces inside existing Profile, Analytics, CV/Portfolio, and
  Opportunity Hub pages; no new top-level routes were required
✓ No orphan modules — every PGI capability above cites the existing section it extends
✓ Backward compatible — `careerStage` is optional on the User schema; existing accounts
  without one default to unset, not to a forced migration
```

### 50.10 Infrastructure, Scalability & Performance Architecture

#### 50.10.0 Positioning

```
This is not a feature — it is the backend engineering standard the rest of this document
already assumes without stating. Performance targets (§2.5), offline behavior (§29), the
four-layer frontend resilience architecture (§34), and the monitoring list (§37.16) all
describe how PROFILE should *behave*; none of them specify the infrastructure that makes
that behavior possible at scale. This section closes that gap — extending, not replacing,
every one of those sections. Every capability in Sections 1–50.9 must remain deliverable
under this architecture without redesign as PROFILE grows from thousands of users to
hundreds of millions.
```

#### 50.10.1 Cloud-Native Deployment Model

```typescript
const DEPLOYMENT_MODEL = {
  applicationServers: 'stateless — any instance can serve any request; session state lives
    in the distributed cache (§50.10.4), never in-process',
  scaling: 'horizontal by default; vertical only where a workload is genuinely not
    horizontally scalable (e.g. certain database primaries)',
  autoScaling: 'triggered on request latency and queue depth, not just CPU',
  regions: 'multi-region from launch-critical services onward (§50.3\'s V1_GO_LIVE_WHITELIST) —
    country verification adapters (§16.6) already imply regional data residency needs;
    the deployment model must not fight that requirement later',
  deploys: ['zero_downtime', 'rolling_updates', 'blue_green', 'canary_releases'],
};
// No capability in this document may assume a single application server or a single
// region without an explicit, documented exception.
```

#### 50.10.2 Load Balancing & Traffic Management

```typescript
const LOAD_BALANCING = {
  layers: ['layer_4', 'layer_7'],
  routing: ['geographic_routing', 'health_checks', 'automatic_failover'],
  sessionAffinity: 'used only where a specific workflow genuinely requires it (e.g. a live
    verification-matching batch job, §16.4) — never as a substitute for stateless design',
};
```

#### 50.10.3 Database Architecture

```
Extends, does not duplicate, §50.2's DATA_DOMAINS — that section separates data by
*sensitivity and audience* (public/private/institutional); this separates data by
*physical/logical store*. The two are complementary dimensions of the same data, not two
competing models.
```

```typescript
const DATABASE_TOPOLOGY = {
  operational:  'primary transactional store — user, activity, verification, commerce records',
  analytics:    'separate store for §23/§40.9 Analytics — never queried directly against
    the operational database for heavy aggregation',
  searchIndex:  'separate store backing §50.10.8 Search Infrastructure',
  logging:      'separate from the operational database — high-volume, append-heavy,
    different retention rules than user data',
  audit:        'separate again from general logging — this is where §37.7 SecurityAuditEntry
    and §50.2 TrustIntegrityLedgerEntry actually live: append-only, immutable, never
    sharing a store with mutable operational data',
  cache:        'see §50.10.4',
  scaling:      ['read_replicas', 'connection_pooling', 'query_optimization',
    'index_optimization', 'partitioning', 'sharding_where_genuinely_needed',
    'automatic_failover', 'online_migrations_no_downtime'],
};
```

#### 50.10.4 Distributed Caching & CDN Strategy

```typescript
const CACHING = {
  layers: ['redis_or_equivalent', 'edge_caching', 'query_cache', 'session_cache', 'object_cache'],
  invalidation: 'automatic, event-driven (§50.10.7) — a Trust Score change, a verification
    approval, or a profile edit invalidates the relevant cache entry as part of that same
    domain event, never left to a time-based expiry alone for user-visible data',
};

const CDN_STRATEGY = {
  servedViaCDN: ['images', 'videos', 'documents', 'javascript', 'css', 'fonts', 'icons',
    'portfolio_assets_(§15.4)', 'marketplace_assets_(§19)', 'public_static_resources'],
  neverServedViaPublicCDN: 'anything gated by §50.10.3\'s private-data-domain rules or
    §37.21\'s signed-URL requirement for private files — a CDN edge is a public cache;
    it must never become an accidental bypass of an authorization check',
};
```

#### 50.10.5 Intelligent File Optimization Pipeline (extends §11.4, §37.21, §49.7a — one
pipeline, not a parallel one)

```
Every file already flows through §37.21's deep validation (type inspection, zip-bomb
limits, malware scanning) before this stage ever runs. This section adds what happens
after a file is confirmed safe:
```

```typescript
const FILE_OPTIMIZATION_PIPELINE = [
  'validate', 'virus_scan',           // already required by §27.2/§37.21 — restated for
                                        // pipeline ordering, not re-specified
  'optimize', 'compress', 'generate_previews', 'generate_thumbnails', 'generate_metadata',
  'extract_searchable_text_where_applicable',   // feeds §50.10.8 Search Infrastructure
  'generate_streaming_version', 'generate_mobile_version', 'generate_web_version',
  'preserve_original',                 // always — required for credential documents (§4.7)
                                        // and any legally/professionally significant upload
];
// Compression targets significant storage reduction with no perceptible quality loss for
// professional use — modern codecs, not aggressive lossy compression that would undermine
// a credential document's evidentiary value (§12.5 Credential Vault).
```

#### 50.10.6 Background Processing & Queue Architecture

```typescript
const BACKGROUND_WORKLOADS = [
  'video_processing', 'image_optimization', 'ai_analysis_(§48.11_AI_Gateway)',
  'portfolio_and_cv_generation_(§15)', 'verification_processing_(§16.4)',
  'notifications', 'emails', 'search_indexing', 'analytics_aggregation',
  'trust_score_calculation_(§13)', 'report_generation', 'document_parsing', 'ocr',
  'virus_scanning',
];
const QUEUE_ARCHITECTURE = {
  features: ['retry_logic', 'dead_letter_queues', 'priority_queues', 'scheduled_jobs',
    'batch_processing', 'event_driven_workflows'],
  rule: 'no long-running operation blocks a user-facing request — a CV regeneration after
    a new verification (§5.3 auto-update triggers, <2s target per §2.5) is perceived as
    fast because it is queued and streamed back, not because it runs synchronously',
};
```

#### 50.10.7 Event-Driven Architecture

```typescript
const DOMAIN_EVENTS = [
  'user_registered', 'activity_created', 'verification_approved', 'portfolio_updated',
  'trust_score_changed', 'marketplace_sale_(§38.6b)', 'reference_issued_(§40.5)',
  'reference_accepted_(§40.5.1)', 'organisation_joined_(§42.3)', 'affiliation_confirmed',
  'direct_pitch_sent_(§39.13)',
];
// These events are the actual mechanism behind cache invalidation (§50.10.4), the Daily
// Return Engine's freshness (§11.0), and every "X now appears on Y" rule already stated
// throughout this document (e.g. §5.2's Feature Linkage Table) — this section names the
// event bus as the thing that makes those existing rules real, not a new set of rules.
```

#### 50.10.8 Search Infrastructure at Scale (extends §17 Discover & Smart Search)

```
§17.3 already defines what Smart Search must do — natural language, typo tolerance,
semantic ranking, and the full index list (people, organisations, jobs, skills, tools,
research, marketplace, and more). This adds the infrastructure requirement underneath it:
search must stay performant at global scale (§2.5-equivalent target: <500ms), backed by
its own dedicated search index (§50.10.3), kept current via the event bus (§50.10.7) rather
than batch-rebuilt, and — per §17.1's Hard Rule, restated at the infrastructure level — the
ranking signals available to the search index are exactly the verified, trust-based signals
§17.2 already specifies, never a raw engagement metric the index happens to have handy.
```

#### 50.10.9 Performance Targets — Infrastructure Layer (extends §2.5, which covers the
client/frontend side only)

```typescript
const INFRASTRUCTURE_PERFORMANCE_TARGETS = {
  apiResponseTypicalP95:   '< 200ms',      // restates §2.5 at the infra level it actually depends on
  searchLatency:           '< 500ms',
  messageDeliveryLatency:  'near real-time',
  notificationLatency:     '< 2s',
  backgroundJobs:          'processed asynchronously — never counted against page-load or
    API-response targets, per §50.10.6\'s queueing rule',
};
// Every number in §2.5 (FCP, TTI, CV auto-update <2s, Trust Score update <1s) is a
// commitment this infrastructure layer exists specifically to keep — these targets are
// the backend half of the same promise, not a second, competing set of numbers.
```

#### 50.10.10 Observability (extends §37.16, which listed monitoring categories with no depth)

```typescript
const OBSERVABILITY_STACK = {
  metrics:            'infrastructure, application, database, and API metrics, per §37.16\'s
    existing list — now with the requirement that they feed the same alerting pipeline,
    not four disconnected dashboards',
  distributedTracing: 'a single request touching application server, queue, database, and
    search index (§50.10.1–§50.10.8) must be traceable end to end as one trace, not
    reconstructed after the fact from separate logs',
  structuredLogging:  'machine-parseable, feeding the redacted-log requirement already
    stated at §37.8 — never raw secrets or full credential documents in log output',
  healthChecks:       'per-service, feeding §50.10.2\'s load balancer failover decisions directly',
  dashboards:         ['performance', 'security_(§37)', 'infrastructure', 'application',
    'database', 'api', 'ai_(§48.11_AI_Gateway_call_logging)'],
};
```

#### 50.10.11 Capacity Planning

```
The architecture in §50.10.1–§50.10.10 must comfortably support millions of registered
users, millions of daily active users, millions of concurrent API requests, large-scale
document and media uploads, enterprise/government/university deployments (§8.4's hub
routing), global marketplace volume (§19, §38), high-volume messaging (§20), and the bulk
verification workloads §16.3 already describes at "50,000 students" scale — without
architectural redesign. This is a design constraint checked against at every stage of
§50.10, not a separate initiative to revisit later.
```

#### 50.10.12 Infrastructure Security (extends §27.3 Active Defence, §37.19's SSRF/external-
API boundaries — closes the remaining infra-layer items, not a duplicate security section)

```typescript
const INFRASTRUCTURE_SECURITY = [
  'WAF', 'ddos_protection',   // §27.3 already lists ddos_mitigation_architecture — this
                               // names the mechanism (WAF) that implements it
  'rate_limiting',            // already required platform-wide per §37.1; restated as an
                               // infra-layer control, not a second policy
  'bot_detection',            // extends §50.1's bot_account_signals risk signal to the
                               // network layer, not just the account-behaviour layer
  'zero_trust_networking', 'encryption_at_rest', 'encryption_in_transit',   // §27.2, unchanged
  'secrets_management', 'key_rotation',   // extends §37.6's "never commit secrets" rule
                               // with the operational practice that enforces it continuously
  'secure_backups', 'tested_disaster_recovery',   // extends §37.16's automated_backups
                               // with the explicit requirement that recovery is tested,
                               // not merely assumed to work
  'immutable_audit_logs',     // already required — §37.7, §50.2's TrustIntegrityLedgerEntry
  'continuous_security_monitoring',   // extends §37.16
];
// Every item here either names the mechanism behind an existing requirement or extends
// an existing one to the infrastructure layer — nothing here is a second, parallel
// security model to the one already governing Sections 27 and 37.
```

#### 50.10.13 Future Expansion Principles

```
This architecture must absorb the following without a major redesign, because each is
already implied by content elsewhere in this document, not hypothetical: additional AI
providers (§48.11 AI Gateway already anticipates this), the developer API ecosystem
(§16.7, §38.10), third-party integrations, additional payment providers (§38.6), additional
storage/search/messaging providers, new regional deployments (§16.6's country-adapter
pattern already assumes this), and future PROFILE products built on this same
infrastructure. The test for whether §50.10.1–§50.10.12 is sufficient is whether each of
these can be added as configuration and integration work — never as a rebuild of the
database topology, caching layer, or event bus underneath them.
```

#### 50.10.13a Provider Abstraction Layer & Engineering Law #1 (new — makes §50.10.13's
"configuration work, never a rebuild" claim concrete with actual adapter interfaces;
extends §48.11 AI Gateway's pattern to every other infrastructure provider)

```
ENGINEERING LAW #1: No business logic, domain logic, or user-facing feature may depend
directly on a third-party infrastructure provider. Every database, identity system,
storage backend, payment processor, search engine, messaging/notification service, and AI
model is accessed exclusively through a PROFILE-owned adapter interface — never through a
vendor SDK called directly from feature code. This is the same discipline §48.11 already
requires for AI providers specifically ("no feature calls a model provider's SDK
directly"); this law generalises it to every provider category, platform-wide.

PROFILE is a platform that CURRENTLY USES a given set of infrastructure vendors — it is
never itself "a Supabase application," "a Stripe application," or "an OpenAI application."
A vendor sits at the bottom of the stack, behind an adapter, never in the middle of it.
Application services (§50.10.14's module boundaries) call PROFILE-owned repository and
provider interfaces; they never know or care which vendor answers underneath. Replacing a
vendor is a configuration and adapter-implementation change, never an application rewrite —
this is §50.10.18's Final Infrastructure Principle, stated here as the specific mechanism
that makes it true for infrastructure providers rather than only as a general aspiration.
```

```typescript
// The six adapter interfaces every infrastructure category is accessed through. §48.11's
// AIProvider/AI Gateway already exists and is unchanged — listed here for completeness so
// all six live in one place.
interface DatabaseAdapter { /* ProfileRepository, TrustRepository, UserRepository, etc. —
  domain-shaped repositories, never raw vendor query builders exposed to feature code */ }
interface IdentityProvider { /* backs §7's auth flows — today: managed auth service */ }
interface StorageProvider { /* backs §37's media pipeline and §49.7a's capture layer —
  today: managed object storage; decides among vendor backends transparently to callers */ }
interface PaymentProvider { /* backs §38.6's Commerce Engine and §38.6d's Order/Invoice/
  Subscription schemas — today: managed payment processor */ }
interface SearchProvider { /* backs §17.3 Smart Search and §50.10.8's search architecture */ }
interface NotificationProvider { /* backs §20 Messaging and in-app/email/push/SMS delivery —
  routes to whichever channel-specific vendor is configured, transparently to callers */ }
// AIProvider is §48.11's existing AI Gateway — not redefined here.

const ENGINEERING_LAW_1_ENFORCEMENT = [
  'code_review_(§50.11.2)_blocks_any_direct_vendor_SDK_import_outside_the_six_adapter_
   implementations_themselves',
  'every_module_in_§50.10.14\'s_boundary_table_is_audited_for_this_at_every_
   §50.11.5_Production_Readiness_Review',
  'Phase_-1_(§50.11.0a)_documents_which_vendor_currently_backs_each_adapter_—_the_adapter_
   choice_is_a_configuration_record,_never_hardcoded_in_application_code',
];
```

```typescript
// INFRASTRUCTURE EVOLUTION ROADMAP — the staged path each adapter's backing vendor is
// expected to travel as PROFILE scales, matching §50.10.11's capacity-planning targets and
// §50.11.1's DESIGN_SCALE_TARGETS. This is documentation of an evolution PATH, not a
// commitment to build every stage — later stages are entered only when the capacity
// numbers actually require them (§50.10.11), never speculatively.
const INFRASTRUCTURE_EVOLUTION_STAGES = [
  { stage: 1, name: 'Launch',              approach: 'managed_services_behind_all_six_adapters' },
  { stage: 2, name: 'Scale',                approach: 'dedicated_database_instances' },
  { stage: 3, name: 'Regional Scaling',     approach: 'read_replicas_(§50.10.3)' },
  { stage: 4, name: 'Distributed Scaling',  approach: 'partitioning_and_sharding_(§50.10.3)' },
  { stage: 5, name: 'Geo Distribution',     approach: 'regional_clusters_(§50.10.1\'s_multi-region)' },
  { stage: 6, name: 'Planet Scale',         approach: 'custom_distributed_data_platform' },
  { stage: 7, name: 'PROFILE Data Fabric',  approach: 'purpose-built_stores_for_Trust_(§13),_
     the_Trust_Graph_(§9.1),_AI_memory_(§48.11),_the_event_bus_(§50.10.7),_and_analytics_
     (§40.9)_—_each_still_reached_exclusively_through_its_existing_adapter_interface' },
];
// At every stage, the adapter interfaces above do not change — only what's configured
// behind them does. A module written against DatabaseAdapter at Stage 1 requires zero
// code changes to keep working at Stage 7.
```

#### 50.10.14 Modular Service Boundaries (extends §50.7 Feature Routing Matrix — the
boundaries already exist as a routing table; this states them as architecture)

```
§50.7 already assigns every feature in this document to exactly one primary module —
Profile, Trust, Verification, Career, Messaging, Media, Marketplace, Opportunity,
Intelligence, Community, Admin, Analytics, Security, Privacy, Monetization, Infrastructure,
System Validation. That table is not merely documentation — it is this platform's service
boundary map. Each row is a candidate for an independently deployable service (Identity,
Trust, Verification, Portfolio/CV, Activity, Marketplace, Opportunity, Messaging,
Notification, AI, Search, Analytics, Learning, Payment, Institution Hub, Recruiter Hub,
Organisation Hub, Developer Platform, Admin Platform, and so on), whether PROFILE launches
as a modular monolith or as separated services from day one.

The binding rule either way: modules communicate through the API contracts already implied
by §16.7's VXI APIs, §38.10's licensable APIs, and §48.11's AI Gateway pattern — never
through direct database access across module boundaries, even inside a monolith. A module
built this way can be extracted into its own service later with no change to any other
module's code, only to its deployment configuration. No module in §50.7's table may be
tightly coupled to another's internal implementation, only to its published contract.
```

#### 50.10.15 API Governance & Versioning

```typescript
const API_GOVERNANCE = {
  versioning:       'every API is versioned (e.g. /api/v1/...) — a breaking change ships as
    a new version, never as a silent change to an existing one',
  documentation:    'required for every endpoint, internal or public — §16.7\'s VXI APIs,
    §38.10\'s licensable APIs, and internal service-to-service APIs (§50.10.14) all follow
    the same documentation standard, not a lighter one for internal use',
  idempotency:      'required wherever §37.20\'s Race Conditions & Idempotent Financial Flows
    already demands it, and as a default expectation for any state-changing endpoint',
  pagination:       'required on every list-returning endpoint at scale (search results,
    §50.10.8; candidate pools, §39.6; marketplace listings, §19)',
  rateLimiting:     'per §37.1, now stated as an API-governance requirement, not only a
    security one',
  monitoring:       'every API call feeds §50.10.10\'s Observability stack — API monitoring
    was already listed there; this is what makes that monitoring possible in practice',
  auditLogging:     'per §37.7 — no exception for internal-only APIs',
  backwardCompatibility: 'a new API version does not remove a prior version out from under
    an integration (§16.7\'s institutional integrations, §38.10\'s licensees) without a
    documented deprecation window',
};
// Internal APIs (module-to-module, §50.10.14) follow every rule above exactly like public
// APIs — there is no lower internal standard that later becomes a migration problem when
// a module is extracted into its own service.
```

#### 50.10.16 Graceful Degradation & Service Priority Tiers

```typescript
// ASSUMED FAILURE DOMAINS — the specific threats §34's frontend resilience layers and the
// SERVICE_PRIORITY_TIERS below are jointly designed to survive. Naming these explicitly,
// in one place, closes the one real gap this subsection previously left implicit.
const ASSUMED_FAILURE_DOMAINS = [
  'application_server_failure', 'database_failure', 'cache_failure', 'network_failure',
  'traffic_spikes', 'regional_outages', 'hardware_failures',
  'third_party_api_failures', 'ai_service_failures_(§48.11_AI_Gateway)',
  'payment_failures_(§38.6b)',
];
// PROFILE assumes every one of these will happen, not might happen. No single point of
// failure may exist for any core-tier service below. §34's four-layer frontend resilience
// architecture and this subsection's backend service-priority tiering are the two halves
// of the same answer to this same list — not two separate resilience models.

const SERVICE_PRIORITY_TIERS = {
  core: ['authentication', 'profile_access', 'messaging_(§20)', 'verification_(§16)', 'core_apis'],
        // must remain available during any regional outage, traffic spike, or partial
        // infrastructure failure (§50.10's resilience posture) — these are never deferred
  deferrable: ['ai_processing_(§48.11)', 'analytics_(§23,_§40.9)', 'recommendations_(§48.8-48.9)',
        'media_optimization_(§50.10.5)', 'search_indexing_(§50.10.8)'],
        // may be queued, delayed, or temporarily disabled under peak load without the
        // platform being considered down — a person can still verify their identity, message
        // a recruiter, and view their profile even if AI recommendations are running behind
};
// This is the concrete version of §34's existing Four-Layer Resilience Architecture applied
// to backend service prioritization specifically — §34 already establishes that PROFILE must
// never show a blank screen; this section establishes which services keep that promise first
// when everything cannot run at full capacity simultaneously.
```

#### 50.10.17 Privacy-by-Design Module Checklist (extends §25/§25.1/§27.4 — a per-module
completeness check, not a new privacy model)

```
Every module in §50.7's routing table must explicitly define, before it ships: data
ownership, visibility rules (§25.1's PrivacyMode / §9.7's Private Professional Mode pattern),
download permissions, sharing permissions, verification permissions (§16), export
permissions (§27.4's userRights, §25's DataRights tab), retention policy, archival policy,
and legal hold support (§26's legal_hold_management). A module missing any of these eight
is not launch-ready regardless of how complete its user-facing functionality is — this is
now a checklist item in §37.25's Final Platform Validation Checklist, not a separate gate.
```

#### 50.10.18 Final Infrastructure Principle

```
This principle governs §50.10.1–§50.10.17 as a whole, and is the standard against which
any future infrastructure decision for PROFILE should be checked:

  Every module, service, infrastructure component, storage provider, AI model, search
  engine, payment provider, messaging system, and integration must be replaceable with
  minimal impact on the rest of the platform.

§50.10.14's module boundaries, §48.11's AI Gateway, §50.10.15's versioned API contracts,
and §50.3's staged activation model are each, independently, an application of this one
rule to a different layer of the stack. This is what keeps PROFILE maintainable, scalable,
and adaptable for decades of continuous evolution rather than requiring a rebuild every
time a vendor, provider, or dependency changes underneath it.
```

---

### 50.11 Production Excellence Framework (PREF)

#### 50.11.0 Positioning

```
§37 governs security controls. §50.10 governs infrastructure architecture. Neither governs
the PROCESS by which PROFILE is designed, built, tested, launched, and operated over its
lifetime — the methodology layer that decides when a feature is actually done, not just
built. PREF is that layer. It is organised as five stages plus one prerequisite gate
(Phase -1) and a standing enterprise-procurement checklist, and it governs every feature in
this document the same way §35's Activation Audit Requirements already governs completeness
and §37.25's Final Platform Validation Checklist already governs release safety — PREF is
the parent process both of those already operate inside, made explicit for the first time.

RULE: nothing in §50.11 re-specifies a control that §27, §37, or §50.10 already define. Where
this section names an existing control (encryption, RLS, MFA, the AI Gateway), it does so
only to place that control at the correct stage of the development lifecycle — the control
itself is never redefined here.
```

#### 50.11.0a Phase -1 — Production Architecture & Governance Gate (precedes Phase 0 of any build sequence, including §49.9's Recommended Build Sequence)

```
Before implementation begins on PROFILE or any major module addition, Phase -1 must
produce and document: the architectural decisions this document already contains (§2 stack,
§50.10 infrastructure topology), the security baseline (§27, §37), the scalability model
(§50.10.11 Capacity Planning), the compliance requirements (§27.4, §46), AI governance
(§48.0's Hard Rules, §48.11's Gateway), and the quality gates below (§50.11.4–50.11.5).
§49.9's Recommended Build Sequence already orders WHAT gets built first; Phase -1 is the
gate confirming those architectural decisions exist and are documented BEFORE step 1 of
that sequence starts, not a competing build order. Every subsequent development phase must
be checked against Phase -1's documented decisions before it can be marked complete — a
phase that silently contradicts an established decision (e.g. introducing a stateful
service against §50.10.1's stateless-by-default rule) fails Phase -1 review regardless of
how complete its user-facing functionality is.
```

#### 50.11.1 Stage 0 — Strategy & Architecture (extends §2 Technology Stack, §50.10 —
citation only where already specified)

```
Product architecture principles (vision, product principles/constraints, domain-driven
design, API-first, mobile-first, offline-first, AI-first, Zero Trust) are already
established: vision/mission at §43.1–43.2, mobile-first at §2.3/§10.0, offline-first at
§29.2, AI-first at §48.0a, Zero Trust at §50.10.12/§37.19. Technical architecture choices
(frontend/backend/database/CDN/cache/search/queue/observability/CI/CD/IaC/disaster
recovery) are already named at §2.1 and detailed at §50.10.1–50.10.12. Scalability
architecture (horizontal/vertical scaling, stateless servers, sharding, geo-replication,
edge computing, multi-region) is already fully specified at §50.10.1–50.10.4 and
§50.10.11's capacity target.

GENUINE ADDITION — explicit design-scale targets, not previously stated as numbers:
```

```typescript
const DESIGN_SCALE_TARGETS = {
  dailyActiveUsers:          '1,000,000+',
  registeredUsers:           '100,000,000+',
  records:                    '10,000,000,000+',
  concurrentRealtimeConnections: 'millions (§20 Messaging, §50.10.7 event bus)',
};
// These are DESIGN targets, checked at Phase -1 and re-checked at every §50.11.5
// Production Readiness Review — not a claim about current traffic. §50.10.11's Capacity
// Planning already states the qualitative requirement ("millions of registered users...
// without architectural redesign"); this gives it the specific numbers Phase -1 architects
// against, so "at scale" has a concrete target rather than remaining open-ended.

const DDD_PATTERNS_AT_SCALE = ['bounded_context_mapping', 'CQRS_where_genuinely_needed',
  'event_sourcing_where_applicable', 'fan_out_architecture_for_high_fanout_reads_(e.g._
  activity_feed_§11)', 'streaming_architecture_(§50.10.7\'s_event_bus)'];
// These are optional patterns available WITHIN §50.10.14's existing module boundaries —
// applied per-module where genuinely needed (e.g. CQRS for the Activity feed's high
// read/write asymmetry), never a mandate to rebuild every module this way.
```

#### 50.11.2 Stage 1 — Foundation Engineering (extends §37.22 Dependency/Supply Chain/
Deployment Hardening — most items already specified there; genuine additions only)

```typescript
// Already specified at §37.22 and cross-referenced, not restated: dependency scanning,
// deployment hardening, secrets management (§37.6), feature flags (§50.3's FeatureFlag),
// database migrations with no-downtime online migrations (§50.10.3), observability/
// telemetry/metrics/tracing/logging/alerting (§50.10.10).

// GENUINE ADDITIONS — engineering process standards not previously named:
const FOUNDATION_ENGINEERING_STANDARDS = [
  'documented_branching_strategy', 'enforced_code_standards_and_linting',
  'required_code_review_before_merge', 'documented_testing_framework_(§50.11.4)',
  'environment_management_(dev/staging/production_parity)',
  'documented_rollback_strategy_per_service_(extends_§50.10.1\'s_zero_downtime_deploys)',
  'semantic_versioning_for_every_internal_and_public_API_(§50.10.15)',
];
// RULE: no module (§50.10.14's module boundaries) is considered foundation-complete until
// every item above is documented for it — this is the literal gate §50.11.0a checks for.
```

#### 50.11.3 Stage 2 — Secure Development Lifecycle (extends §27, §37.1–37.24, §46.4 —
most controls already specified; genuine addition is the threat-modeling methodology itself)

```
Encryption (TLS everywhere, AES-256 at rest, key rotation, encrypted backups) is already
required platform-wide at §27.2 and §50.10.12. Identity (MFA, passkeys, biometrics, device
trust, risk-based/step-up authentication, impossible-travel detection, session
fingerprinting) is already specified at §7.2, §37.3, §37.26.1, and §50.2's
MFA_POLICY_BY_RISK_TIER. Privacy (GDPR/CCPA/NDPR/UK GDPR/PIPEDA, data residency, retention,
right to delete/export, consent management, anonymisation) is already specified at §27.4,
§37.26.10, §46.4, and §50.3's RETENTION_AND_DELETION_POLICY. OWASP Top 10-aligned controls
(secure headers, rate limiting, CSRF/XSS/SQLi/SSRF/command injection prevention, file
upload security, malware scanning) are already required at §27.2, §37.19, and §37.21.
None of this is re-specified below.

GENUINE ADDITION — formal threat-modeling methodology. Nothing in §27/§37 previously named
HOW threats are identified, only which controls result from having identified them:
```

```typescript
const THREAT_MODELING_METHODOLOGY = {
  framework: 'STRIDE',   // Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information disclosure,
                          // Denial of service, Elevation of privilege
  requiredArtifacts: ['attack_trees', 'misuse_cases', 'documented_trust_boundaries',
    'risk_register', 'data_classification_(maps_directly_onto_§50.2\'s_existing_
    DATA_DOMAINS: public/private/institutional)'],
  cadence: 'required at Phase -1 for any new module, and re-run whenever a module\'s trust
    boundary changes (e.g. a new third-party integration, per §37.19)',
  outputFeedsInto: 'the RISK_SIGNALS catalog already defined at §50.1 — a STRIDE exercise
    that identifies a new threat class adds a signal there, it does not create a second
    risk-tracking system',
};

// Named framework conformance targets — extends §27's OWASP Top 10 baseline with the
// more specific standards this patch calls out by name:
const SECURITY_FRAMEWORK_CONFORMANCE = ['OWASP_ASVS', 'OWASP_API_Security_Top_10', 'OWASP_MASVS'];
// Conformance level (ASVS Level 1/2/3) is a Phase -1 architectural decision, documented
// once, not re-litigated per feature — consistent with §50.11.0's governing rule.
```

standard did not previously exist anywhere in this document)

```typescript
  'network_interruption', 'battery_optimisation_(mobile)', 'memory_leak_testing',
];

const COVERAGE_TARGETS = {
  standardServices: '90%+',
  criticalServices:  '95%+',    // §50.10.16's core-tier services: auth, profile access,
                                  // messaging, verification, core APIs
  securityCriticalPaths: '100%', // auth, payment (§38.6d), credential verification (§16)
};
// A feature does not pass §37.25's Final Platform Validation Checklist without meeting the

```

Validation Checklist — this is that checklist's formal review process, not a second gate)

```typescript
  'compliance_(§27.4,_§46)',
];
```

```
Monitoring stack (metrics, structured logging, distributed tracing, health checks, RUM,
```

```typescript
  slo:     string;                 // e.g. "< 200ms for 99.9% of requests" — reuses

}

// SLOs are set for §50.10.16's core tier first (auth, profile access, messaging,
// verification, core APIs) — this is the operational enforcement of that section's
// existing "never deferred" rule, not a new priority list.
```

#### 50.11.8 PROFILE-Specific Additions (added by the pre-Phase-7 patch — these seven
items close gaps this document named but never field-specified, or specified as pieces
without assembling into a named surface; each is cross-referenced to the section it extends,
not a new independent system)

```
1. CV header contact fields — §4.1's header never listed location, LinkedIn URL, or an
   external website field. Added as real, user-entered, optional fields on the individual
   account (accountTypes.ts's IndividualAccount) and rendered on every CV template.

2. CV sub-schema field specification — §4.6 names SummaryContent, EducationEntry,
   WorkEntry, CertificationEntry, LanguageEntry, and a references concept without ever
   specifying their fields. Field-specified here: summary statement, years of experience,
   key achievement, and an objective-mode flag; education (degree, school, graduation year,
   optional GPA shown only per the GPA display rule below, relevant coursework);
   certifications (name, issuer, year); languages (language, proficiency level); and a
   self-reported external-reference list (name, job title, company, relationship, contact
   details) that sits alongside, and never replaces, §47.2/§47.9's institutional
   ProfessionalReference verification flow.

3. GPA display rule — a GPA is shown on a CV only if it is 3.5 or above, or the entry's
   graduation year is within the last two years. This is a client-side rendering rule over
   a value the user themselves entered — never a fabricated or suppressed fact (§48.0/§49.8).

4. Work entry location — §4.6's WorkEntry gained an optional per-entry location field,
   populated only when the user supplies one when recording the underlying Activity
   (§15.1); left blank otherwise rather than inferred.

5. Portfolio Organisation composability — §15.2's Organisation / Company Showcase
   template, previously a fixed four-block layout, is now a composable system: a named,
   toggleable section list (header, hero, about us, services, projects, team, clients &
   partners, testimonials, resources, careers, contact, footer) an organisation account can
   turn on or off per section, editable from the same screen the template renders on. Header
   and footer are structural and cannot be hidden.

6. Store Hub — §19's Marketplace listing types, §38.4–§38.5's verified business
   directory, and §38.6a's two-toggle Commerce Activation mechanism existed as separate
   pieces with no single named screen. Store Hub assembles them into one route with a
   Market (always visible, browse-only) and a Create Store section (visible once Commerce
   & Selling is activated in Settings).

7. Owner Content Console and Owner/Admin roles — a staff-only composer for the news,
   blog, and announcement content that feeds §31/§37's public Blog & News surface (which
   this build previously had no write path for at all), plus a second internal role tier:
   Owner, who can create, scope, and revoke Admin accounts limited to specific internal
   tools (mirroring the spirit of §42.2's OrgAdminRoleV3 permission matrix, but for
   PROFILE's own internal staff, never merged with that organisation-facing model). Access
   to the whole Owner/Internal area additionally requires a secondary passphrase on top of
   the normal staff-role check.
```

---

### 50.12 PROFILE Mission Control (PMC) — Owner Intelligence Layer

#### 50.12.0 Positioning

```
PROFILE has three flagship systems, not two: the Trust Center (§12), PROFILE Copilot
(§48.0a), and PROFILE Mission Control. Where Copilot is the intelligence layer a USER
experiences, Mission Control is the intelligence layer the PLATFORM OWNER experiences — the
operational nervous system that continuously watches infrastructure, trust signals, user
health, growth, security, and business performance, and turns that telemetry into a small
number of prioritised decisions rather than an unreadable flood of dashboards and alerts.

Mission Control is not a new data source. Every signal it surfaces already exists
somewhere in this document — §50.10.10's observability stack, §50.1's RISK_SIGNALS, §50.9.3's
Profile Health Dashboard, §40.9's Analytics, §38.19's Revenue Matrix, §37.16's production
monitoring, §50.11.6's SRE/support telemetry. Mission Control's actual job, and its only
genuinely new capability, is SYNTHESIS: combining those existing signals into daily
briefings, explaining WHY a number moved rather than only reporting THAT it moved, and
deciding what deserves the owner's attention today rather than surfacing everything.
```

#### 50.12.1 Intelligence Domains (each domain is a synthesis view over an existing data
source — no domain below introduces new telemetry collection)

```typescript
const MISSION_CONTROL_DOMAINS: Record<string, string> = {
  'Growth Intelligence':       'funnels, cohorts, retention, and churn signals — synthesises
                                 §40.9 Analytics Evolution and §8.10\'s Progress Tracker
                                 completion data across the user base, not per-user',
  'User Health Intelligence':  'aggregates §50.9.3\'s ProfileHealthScores across the user
                                 base into a population-level health view — the per-user
                                 score is unchanged, this is its rollup',
  'Trust Intelligence':        'synthesises §50.1\'s RISK_SIGNALS and §50.5\'s Trust Timeline
                                 activity into fraud/anomaly trend lines',
  'Enterprise Intelligence':   'synthesises §50.11.6.4\'s Enterprise Procurement Readiness
                                 documents and §38.18\'s Enterprise Solutions usage into
                                 procurement-stage, renewal-risk, and expansion signals',
  'Copilot Intelligence':      'synthesises §50.11.6.1\'s AI_OPERATIONS_COPILOT_SCOPE
                                 telemetry (accuracy, latency, cost, safety incidents) that
                                 already flows through §48.11\'s AI Gateway',
  'Infrastructure Intelligence': 'synthesises §50.10.10\'s Observability stack (metrics,
                                 tracing, health checks, dashboards) — no new monitoring',
  'Security Intelligence':     'synthesises §37.16\'s production monitoring and §50.1\'s
                                 anomaly detection into a proactive, pre-incident view',
  'Business Intelligence':     'synthesises §38.19\'s Revenue Stream Matrix and §38.6d\'s
                                 Order/Invoice/Subscription records into MRR/ARR/LTV/CAC and
                                 forecasting views',
  'Operational Intelligence':  'synthesises §50.11.2\'s deployment/CI-CD data and §50.11.6.3\'s
                                 SRE error-budget state',
  'Customer Intelligence':     'synthesises §37.26.9/§50.11.6.2\'s support-ticket data and
                                 §45\'s FAQ gap patterns into sentiment and satisfaction views',
};
```

#### 50.12.2 Daily Executive Briefing (new — the one genuinely new user-facing artifact)

```typescript
interface DailyBriefing {
  date: Date;
  greeting: string;                        // "Good morning."
  keyMetrics: { label: string; value: string; delta?: string }[];
                                            // e.g. "18,429 new users", "Trust Score +0.6%" —
                                            // each value traceable to its domain in §50.12.1
  notableSignals: string[];                // plain-language observations, not raw numbers —
                                            // "Job Marketplace engagement fell 7%" not a chart
  recommendations: {
    text: string;
    estimatedImpact?: string;              // e.g. "+$420,000 ARR" — always labelled as an
                                            // estimate per §48.0's AI Transparency rule,
                                            // never presented as a guaranteed outcome
    governingSection?: string;             // e.g. links a SAML request pattern to §38.18
  }[];
}
// Delivered as a §48.0a Copilot skill output ("Owner Copilot," §50.12.3) — not a second
// AI system, and bound by the same §48.0 Hard Rules: never fabricates a metric, always
// distinguishes a measured fact from a forecasted estimate.
```

#### 50.12.3 AI Executive Board (extends §48.0a's Copilot Skills Framework with
owner-facing domain skills — same personality, same Hard Rules, same CopilotContext pattern,
new skill names)

```typescript
// EXTENDS §48.0a's COPILOT_SKILLS table — these are additional rows, not a second
// framework. Each "Copilot" below is PROFILE Copilot operating with an owner-scoped
// context and permission set (§37.6 RBAC — owner/admin roles only), synthesising the
// domain listed at §50.12.1, never collecting new telemetry of its own.
const MISSION_CONTROL_EXECUTIVE_SKILLS: Record<string, string> = {
  'CEO Copilot':                 'combines every domain below into the Daily Briefing (§50.12.2)',
  'CTO Copilot':                 'Infrastructure Intelligence + Operational Intelligence',
  'CISO Copilot':                'Security Intelligence + Trust Intelligence',
  'CFO Copilot':                 'Business Intelligence',
  'COO Copilot':                 'Operational Intelligence + Customer Intelligence',
  'Chief Trust Officer Copilot': 'Trust Intelligence (§50.1) in full detail, for governance
                                   decisions beyond the CISO Copilot\'s security framing',
  'Chief Community Officer Copilot': 'Customer Intelligence + §21.2 Communities & Groups activity',
};
// RULE: each executive skill only synthesises and recommends — it never takes a
// consequential action autonomously. Any action a briefing recommends that would touch
// user data, money, or trust standing goes through the existing human-confirmation gates
// already defined for that action elsewhere (§48.0's Hard Rules, §38.6b's server-side-only
// commission computation, §50.1's dual sign-off for Trust weight changes) — Mission Control
// recommends; it does not acquire new authority to act that the underlying system doesn't
// already grant a human.
```

#### 50.12.4 Owner Prioritisation Engine (the actual differentiator — deciding what
deserves attention, not just reporting everything)

```typescript
interface PriorityDecision {
  title: string;
  severity: 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5;              // stars — 5 = critical
  domain: keyof typeof MISSION_CONTROL_DOMAINS;
  estimatedImpact?: string;
  recommendedOwner: string;                  // a team or role, e.g. "Platform Team"
  estimatedEffort?: string;                  // e.g. "3 hours"
}
const PRIORITISATION_RULE =
  'the_owner_sees_a_small_number_of_ranked_decisions_per_day_(target:_single_digits),_
   never_a_raw_alert_feed_—_every_lower-priority_signal_is_still_fully_logged_and_
   queryable,_it_is_simply_not_pushed_to_the_top_of_the_briefing';
// Ranking logic reuses §50.1's RISK_RESPONSES severity model and §50.11.6.3's SRE error-
// budget framing (a core-tier service, §50.10.16, burning its error budget outranks a
// deferrable-tier signal by definition) — not a second, independent priority scoring system.
```

#### 50.12.5 Governance & Scope Limits

```typescript
const MISSION_CONTROL_HARD_RULES = [
  'introduces_no_new_data_collection_beyond_what_§37.7\'s_audit_log,_§50.10.10\'s_
   observability_stack,_and_this_document\'s_existing_analytics_sections_already_collect',
  'never_exposes_individual_user_data_to_the_owner_beyond_what_§50.2\'s_data_domain_
   separation_already_permits_for_an_administrator_role_(§37.6_RBAC)_—_aggregate_
   population-level_views_by_default,_individual_drill-down_only_where_an_existing_
   admin_permission_already_allows_it',
  'inherits_every_§48.0_AI_Engine_Hard_Rule_unchanged_—_never_fabricates_a_metric,_
   always_labels_a_forecast_as_a_forecast',
  'automation_of_a_recommended_action_is_governed_by_§50.3\'s_ACTIVATION_GATE_—_a_
   playbook_that_would_act_automatically_is_a_hold-back_feature_until_it_passes_that_
   gate_exactly_like_any_other_automation_in_this_document,_never_a_carve-out',
];
```
